# Syllabus

Every wing, shelf and card in the library, in reading order: **25 wings, 255 shelves and 1,866 cards**, from counting to the open problems of current research. Each card uses only ideas from the cards before it, so the library can be read from the first card to the last.

**How to use it:** pick a wing below. Its page starts with a list of its shelves; under that, each shelf lists its cards. **Click a card's title to read it.** A title without a link is planned and not written yet.

**1,087 of 1,866 cards are ready to read.**

## 01 · Foundations

Work fluently with whole numbers, fractions, decimals, percentages, powers, roots and logarithms; handle compound growth and discounting; read and write logic and proofs; and use sets, relations, functions and the sizes of infinity. This is the toolkit every later wing assumes.

9 shelves · **59 of 59 cards ready to read** · [open the folder](Cards/01-Foundations)

### 01 · Everyday Arithmetic · 10 cards

*Whole numbers, the four operations, order of operations, the rearranging laws, negatives, fractions, decimals, ratios, percentages.*

1. [Place value: why 523 means five hundreds, two tens and three ones](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/01-place-value.md)
2. [Adding and subtracting: combining and taking away, with carrying and borrowing](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/02-adding-and-subtracting.md)
3. [Multiplying and dividing: adding in one move, and sharing out what is left](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/03-multiplying-and-dividing.md)
4. [Order of operations: which step goes first, and why brackets exist](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/04-order-of-operations.md)
5. [The three rearranging laws: swapping, regrouping, and spreading multiplication over addition](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/05-arithmetic-laws.md)
6. [Negative numbers: the number line runs both ways](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/06-negative-numbers.md)
7. [Fractions: parts of a whole, and how to add, multiply and divide them](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/07-fractions.md)
8. [Decimals: fractions whose bottom is a power of ten](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/08-decimals.md)
9. [Ratios and rates: comparing two quantities and scaling them together](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/09-ratios-and-rates.md)
10. [Percentages: parts per hundred, rises, cuts, and undoing them](Cards/01-Foundations/01-Everyday%20Arithmetic/10-percentages.md)

2. [The number line and inequalities: left is smaller, and what flips when you multiply by a negative](Cards/01-Foundations/02-The%20Number%20Line/02-number-line-and-inequalities.md)

5. [Logarithms: the question 'what power got me here?'](Cards/01-Foundations/03-Powers%2C%20Roots%20and%20Logarithms/05-logarithms.md)

## 02 · Number theory

Work with divisibility, primes and the greatest common divisor, compute with remainders on a clock, raise numbers to powers modulo n, and see how check digits, calendars and the codes behind secure messages rest on them.

7 shelves · **43 of 43 cards ready to read** · [open the folder](Cards/02-Number%20theory)

| Shelf | Cards |
| --- | ---: |
| [01 · Divisibility and Primes](#w02-s01) | 8 |

| [03 · Clock Arithmetic](#w02-s03) | 6 |
| [04 · Powers on the Clock](#w02-s04) | 6 |
| [05 · Check Digits, Calendars and Cycles](#w02-s05) | 5 |

<a name="w02-s01"></a>

### 01 · Divisibility and Primes · 8 cards

*Divides, factors and multiples, parity, digit rules, division with remainder, primes and trial division, the sieve, factor trees, counting divisors. Needs only multiplying-and-dividing and arithmetic-laws (plus place-value and adding-and-subtracting, which precede them). No negatives, no exponents, no proof cards.*

1. [Divides: when one number goes into another with nothing left over](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/01-divides.md)
2. [Even and odd: the two-way split, and what adding and multiplying do to it](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/02-even-and-odd.md)
3. [Divisibility rules: reading the digits to see whether 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 9 or 10 divides a number](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/03-divisibility-rules.md)
4. [Division with a remainder: the quotient and the leftover are unique, and the leftover is smaller than the divisor](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/04-division-with-remainder.md)
5. [Primes and composites: the numbers that will not split into equal rows, and the ones that will](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/05-primes-and-composites.md)
6. [The sieve of Eratosthenes: cross out every multiple and the primes are what is left standing](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/06-sieve-of-eratosthenes.md)
7. [Prime factorisation: a factor tree breaks any number into prime atoms, and every route reaches the same atoms](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/07-prime-factorisation.md)
8. [Counting divisors: read how many divisors a number has straight off its prime factorisation](Cards/02-Number%20theory/01-Divisibility%20and%20Primes/08-counting-divisors.md)

<a name="w02-s02"></a>

### 02 · Greatest Common Divisor and Euclid's Algorithm · 8 cards

*Shared factors and multiples, Euclid's algorithm, Bezout's mix, coprimality, Euclid's lemma, why factorisation is unique, infinitely many primes. First use of negative numbers, fractions and the proof cards.*

1. [Greatest common divisor: the biggest number that divides both, from factor lists and from prime factorisations](Cards/02-Number%20theory/02-Greatest%20Common%20Divisor%20and%20Euclid%27s%20Algorithm/01-gcd.md)
2. [Least common multiple: the first number both divide into, and why gcd times lcm equals the product](Cards/02-Number%20theory/02-Greatest%20Common%20Divisor%20and%20Euclid%27s%20Algorithm/02-lcm.md)
3. [Euclid's algorithm: divide, keep the remainder, repeat, and the last non-zero remainder is the gcd](Cards/02-Number%20theory/02-Greatest%20Common%20Divisor%20and%20Euclid%27s%20Algorithm/03-euclidean-algorithm.md)
4. [Bezout's identity: the gcd is always a whole-number mix of the two numbers, and running Euclid backwards finds the mix](Cards/02-Number%20theory/02-Greatest%20Common%20Divisor%20and%20Euclid%27s%20Algorithm/04-bezouts-identity.md)
5. [Coprime numbers: sharing no factor but 1, and why that one condition unlocks so much](Cards/02-Number%20theory/02-Greatest%20Common%20Divisor%20and%20Euclid%27s%20Algorithm/05-coprime-numbers.md)
6. [Euclid's lemma: if a prime divides a product it divides one of the factors, and composites carry no such promise](Cards/02-Number%20theory/02-Greatest%20Common%20Divisor%20and%20Euclid%27s%20Algorithm/06-euclids-lemma.md)
7. [Why the factorisation is unique: Euclid's lemma turns the factor tree into the fundamental theorem of arithmetic](Cards/02-Number%20theory/02-Greatest%20Common%20Divisor%20and%20Euclid%27s%20Algorithm/07-unique-factorisation.md)
8. [There are infinitely many primes: multiply the ones you have, add one, and a new prime must exist](Cards/02-Number%20theory/02-Greatest%20Common%20Divisor%20and%20Euclid%27s%20Algorithm/08-infinitude-of-primes.md)

<a name="w02-s03"></a>

### 03 · Clock Arithmetic · 6 cards

*Congruence, adding and multiplying remainders, the n remainder buckets, inverses, solving a x = b on the clock, the Chinese remainder theorem. Needs negatives; no exponents.*

1. [Congruence: two numbers count as the same when they leave the same remainder, written a ≡ b (mod n)](Cards/02-Number%20theory/03-Clock%20Arithmetic/01-congruence-mod-n.md)
2. [Adding and multiplying on the clock: reduce before or after, the answer agrees](Cards/02-Number%20theory/03-Clock%20Arithmetic/02-modular-addition-and-multiplication.md)
3. [Residue classes: the n remainder buckets, and the small addition and multiplication tables they form](Cards/02-Number%20theory/03-Clock%20Arithmetic/03-residue-classes.md)
4. [The modular inverse: dividing on the clock works exactly when the number is coprime to the modulus](Cards/02-Number%20theory/03-Clock%20Arithmetic/04-modular-inverse.md)
5. [Solving a x ≡ b (mod n): when it has answers, how many, and how to find them all](Cards/02-Number%20theory/03-Clock%20Arithmetic/05-linear-congruences.md)
6. [The Chinese remainder theorem: three rotas, one day — day numbers on cycles that share no factor pin down one day, and how to rebuild it](Cards/02-Number%20theory/03-Clock%20Arithmetic/06-chinese-remainder-theorem.md)

<a name="w02-s04"></a>

### 04 · Powers on the Clock · 6 cards

*Fast powers by squaring, Fermat's little theorem, Euler's totient and theorem, orders and primitive roots, Wilson's theorem. First use of exponents from wing 01.*

1. [Powers on the clock: repeated squaring finds 7 to the 123 mod 1000 without ever writing a huge number](Cards/02-Number%20theory/04-Powers%20on%20the%20Clock/01-modular-exponentiation.md)
2. [Fermat's little theorem: raise a number the prime dial does not divide to one below the dial size and it reads 1](Cards/02-Number%20theory/04-Powers%20on%20the%20Clock/02-fermats-little-theorem.md)
3. [Euler's totient: counting how many numbers up to n share no factor with n, straight from the factorisation](Cards/02-Number%20theory/04-Powers%20on%20the%20Clock/03-eulers-totient.md)
4. [Euler's theorem: raise a coprime number to the clock's coprime count and get 1, Fermat set free of primes](Cards/02-Number%20theory/04-Powers%20on%20the%20Clock/04-eulers-theorem.md)
5. [The order of a number and primitive roots: how many steps until the powers return to 1, and the numbers that visit everything](Cards/02-Number%20theory/04-Powers%20on%20the%20Clock/05-order-and-primitive-roots.md)
6. [Wilson's theorem: multiply everything below a prime and the clock shows -1, and only primes do this](Cards/02-Number%20theory/04-Powers%20on%20the%20Clock/06-wilsons-theorem.md)

<a name="w02-s05"></a>

### 05 · Check Digits, Calendars and Cycles · 5 cards

*Where the reader meets remainders: barcodes, ISBNs, the day of the week, cycles that realign, and a card shuffle that resets. Only earlier wing 02 cards needed.*

1. [Barcode check digits: the last digit of an EAN-13 makes a weighted sum land on a multiple of 10](Cards/02-Number%20theory/05-Check%20Digits%2C%20Calendars%20and%20Cycles/01-barcode-check-digit.md)
2. [ISBN-10 and the prime modulus 11: why a check digit on a prime clock also catches two swapped digits](Cards/02-Number%20theory/05-Check%20Digits%2C%20Calendars%20and%20Cycles/02-isbn-check-digit.md)
3. [Day of the week for any date: the calendar is arithmetic mod 7](Cards/02-Number%20theory/05-Check%20Digits%2C%20Calendars%20and%20Cycles/03-day-of-the-week.md)
4. [When cycles meet again: the 60-year calendar, the 52-year calendar round, and why some remainder pairs never happen](Cards/02-Number%20theory/05-Check%20Digits%2C%20Calendars%20and%20Cycles/04-cycles-that-realign.md)
5. [Perfect shuffles: eight perfect riffles restore a 52-card deck, because doubling on a clock of 51 spots comes home in eight](Cards/02-Number%20theory/05-Check%20Digits%2C%20Calendars%20and%20Cycles/05-perfect-shuffles.md)

<a name="w02-s06"></a>

### 06 · Codes and Secrets · 5 cards

*One-way streets, Diffie-Hellman, RSA in outline, and the two primality tests RSA needs. Rests on shelves 2 to 4; no new wing 01 needs beyond exponents.*

1. [One-way streets: multiplying two primes and raising to a power on the clock are easy, and undoing them is slow](Cards/02-Number%20theory/06-Codes%20and%20Secrets/01-one-way-streets.md)
2. [Diffie-Hellman key exchange: two strangers agree a secret over an open line using powers on a clock](Cards/02-Number%20theory/06-Codes%20and%20Secrets/02-diffie-hellman.md)
3. [RSA in outline: lock with e, unlock with d, and Euler's theorem is why the message comes back](Cards/02-Number%20theory/06-Codes%20and%20Secrets/03-rsa-in-outline.md)
4. [The Fermat test: a quick primality check that can be fooled, and the Carmichael numbers that fool it every time](Cards/02-Number%20theory/06-Codes%20and%20Secrets/04-fermat-test-and-carmichael.md)
5. [The Miller-Rabin test: how RSA finds 300-digit primes, with an error as small as you like](Cards/02-Number%20theory/06-Codes%20and%20Secrets/05-miller-rabin.md)

<a name="w02-s07"></a>

### 07 · For the Curious · 5 cards

*Detours with no later dependents: Pythagorean triples, perfect numbers and record primes, how primes thin out, the famous open problems, continued fractions behind the leap-year rule. Analytic proofs live in wing 06.*

1. [Pythagorean triples: every whole-number right triangle comes from two smaller numbers](Cards/02-Number%20theory/07-For%20the%20Curious/01-pythagorean-triples.md)
2. [Perfect numbers and Mersenne primes: 6, 28, 496 and the record-prime hunt](Cards/02-Number%20theory/07-For%20the%20Curious/02-perfect-numbers-and-mersenne.md)
3. [How primes thin out: the prime counting function and the n over log n rule](Cards/02-Number%20theory/07-For%20the%20Curious/03-how-primes-thin-out.md)
4. [Goldbach, twin primes and friends: the simple questions nobody has answered](Cards/02-Number%20theory/07-For%20the%20Curious/04-goldbach-and-open-problems.md)
5. [Continued fractions: Euclid's algorithm read as nested fractions, and why the leap-year rule is 97 in 400](Cards/02-Number%20theory/07-For%20the%20Curious/05-continued-fractions-and-leap-years.md)

## 03 · Algebra

Solve equations, work with polynomials, vectors and matrices, solve linear systems, fit data by least squares, find eigenvalues, and meet the abstract structures behind them: groups, rings and fields.

<a name="w03-s01"></a>

### 01 · Letters and Equations · 4 cards

*Letters standing for numbers, expressions and substitution, solving a linear equation by undoing, inequalities folded in, making a different letter the subject of a formula, two equations in two unknowns by elimination. Needs only wing 01 arithmetic; no exponents beyond squares.*

1. [Letters for numbers: a letter is a number you have not been told yet, and an expression is a recipe that uses one](Cards/03-Algebra/01-Letters%20and%20Equations/01-letters-for-numbers.md)
2. [Linear equations: undo the story step by step, doing the same thing to both sides](Cards/03-Algebra/01-Letters%20and%20Equations/02-linear-equations.md)
3. [Rearranging a formula: making a different letter the subject, so one formula answers many questions](Cards/03-Algebra/01-Letters%20and%20Equations/03-rearranging-formulas.md)
4. [Two equations, two unknowns: substitute or eliminate, and the two ways a crossing fails](Cards/03-Algebra/01-Letters%20and%20Equations/04-two-equations-two-unknowns.md)

<a name="w03-s02"></a>

### 02 · Polynomials · 5 cards

*What a polynomial is, degree and roots, expanding brackets and the square patterns, factoring quadratics, completing the square and the quadratic formula, polynomial long division, the factor theorem and the root ceiling. Needs exponents and roots from wing 01.*

1. [Polynomials: sums of powers of one letter, their degree, and where they cross zero](Cards/03-Algebra/02-Polynomials/01-polynomials.md)
2. [Factoring: un-multiplying a polynomial, because a product is zero only when one factor is](Cards/03-Algebra/02-Polynomials/02-factoring-quadratics.md)
3. [The quadratic formula: complete the square once and every quadratic is solved, and the discriminant says how many answers](Cards/03-Algebra/02-Polynomials/03-quadratic-formula.md)
4. [Polynomial long division: divide with a remainder exactly as with whole numbers, and the remainder is the value at the root](Cards/03-Algebra/02-Polynomials/04-polynomial-division.md)
5. [Roots and factors: a root r means a factor x - r, so a degree-n polynomial has at most n roots](Cards/03-Algebra/02-Polynomials/05-roots-and-the-factor-theorem.md)

<a name="w03-s03"></a>

### 03 · Vectors · 5 cards

*Vectors as lists of numbers and as arrows, adding and scaling, the vector-space rules and subspaces, linear combinations and span, linear independence, basis and dimension with coordinates. No matrices yet; solving small systems by the shelf 1 method.*

1. [Vectors: a list of numbers that is also an arrow, and the two things you can do to it](Cards/03-Algebra/03-Vectors/01-vectors.md)
2. [Vector spaces and subspaces: any collection you can add and scale by the usual rules, and the flat pieces inside it that close up](Cards/03-Algebra/03-Vectors/02-vector-spaces-and-subspaces.md)
3. [Linear combinations and span: everything you can reach by mixing a few vectors](Cards/03-Algebra/03-Vectors/03-linear-combinations-and-span.md)
4. [Linear independence: no vector in the list is a mix of the others, so nothing is redundant](Cards/03-Algebra/03-Vectors/04-linear-independence.md)
5. [Basis and dimension: the smallest set that reaches everything, and the count that never changes](Cards/03-Algebra/03-Vectors/05-basis-and-dimension.md)

<a name="w03-s04"></a>

### 04 · Matrices · 4 cards

*Matrices as tables of numbers, a matrix times a vector as a mix of its columns, matrix multiplication, and a matrix as a linear map.*

1. [Matrices: a table of numbers with a size, plus the named shapes you will keep meeting](Cards/03-Algebra/04-Matrices/01-matrices-and-the-matrix-zoo.md)
2. [Matrix times vector: mix the columns, or run each row along the list, and get the same answer](Cards/03-Algebra/04-Matrices/02-matrix-times-vector.md)
3. [Matrix multiplication: row by column, because it is 'do this, then that', and why the order matters](Cards/03-Algebra/04-Matrices/03-matrix-multiplication.md)
4. [Linear maps: a rule that keeps lines straight and the origin fixed is secretly a matrix, whose columns say where the axes go](Cards/03-Algebra/04-Matrices/04-linear-maps-as-matrices.md)

<a name="w03-s05"></a>

### 05 · Solving Systems · 5 cards

*Ax = b as a system of equations, Gaussian elimination, the inverse matrix, determinants, and rank-nullity.*

1. [Solving A x = b: one answer, no answer or a line of answers, and how to tell which before you start](Cards/03-Algebra/05-Solving%20Systems/01-matrix-equation-ax-b.md)
2. [Gaussian elimination: three legal row moves turn any system into a staircase you can read off from the bottom](Cards/03-Algebra/05-Solving%20Systems/02-gaussian-elimination.md)
3. [The inverse matrix: the matrix that undoes another, the 2 by 2 formula, and when no inverse exists](Cards/03-Algebra/05-Solving%20Systems/03-inverse-matrix.md)
4. [Determinants: the one number that says how much a matrix stretches area, and zero means it squashed something flat](Cards/03-Algebra/05-Solving%20Systems/04-determinants.md)
5. [Rank and nullity: what a matrix keeps and what it kills, and why the two counts add up to the number of inputs](Cards/03-Algebra/05-Solving%20Systems/05-rank-nullity.md)

<a name="w03-s06"></a>

### 06 · Dot Products and Best Fits · 4 cards

*The dot product, length and perpendicularity, projection onto a line and a plane, Gram-Schmidt and orthonormal bases, least squares as projection. Needs roots; the angle is named but trigonometry is not used.*

1. [The dot product: multiply matching entries and add, and one number gives length, perpendicularity and alignment](Cards/03-Algebra/06-Dot%20Products%20and%20Best%20Fits/01-dot-product.md)
2. [Projection: the shadow of one vector on another, and the leftover that is always perpendicular](Cards/03-Algebra/06-Dot%20Products%20and%20Best%20Fits/02-orthogonal-projection.md)
3. [Gram-Schmidt: straighten a skewed basis into perpendicular unit directions, and coordinates become dot products](Cards/03-Algebra/06-Dot%20Products%20and%20Best%20Fits/03-gram-schmidt-and-orthonormal-bases.md)
4. [Least squares: the best-fit line is a projection, and the normal equations hand it to you in one step](Cards/03-Algebra/06-Dot%20Products%20and%20Best%20Fits/04-least-squares.md)

<a name="w03-s07"></a>

### 07 · Eigenvalues and Symmetric Matrices · 6 cards

*Change of basis and similar matrices, eigenvalues and eigenvectors by the 2 by 2 characteristic equation, diagonalisation and fast matrix powers, the spectral theorem for symmetric matrices, quadratic forms and positive definiteness, the singular value decomposition in outline. Rests on shelves 4 and 5.*

1. [Change of basis: the same point with a new address book, and the same map as a new matrix P^-1 A P](Cards/03-Algebra/07-Eigenvalues%20and%20Symmetric%20Matrices/01-change-of-basis.md)
2. [Eigenvalues and eigenvectors: the directions a matrix only stretches, and the stretch factors, found from a quadratic](Cards/03-Algebra/07-Eigenvalues%20and%20Symmetric%20Matrices/02-eigenvalues-and-eigenvectors.md)
3. [Diagonalisation: in the eigenvector basis a matrix only stretches, so A^n is three easy multiplications](Cards/03-Algebra/07-Eigenvalues%20and%20Symmetric%20Matrices/03-diagonalisation-and-matrix-powers.md)
4. [The spectral theorem: a symmetric matrix has real stretch factors along perpendicular axes, A = Q D Q^T](Cards/03-Algebra/07-Eigenvalues%20and%20Symmetric%20Matrices/04-spectral-theorem.md)
5. [Quadratic forms: x^T A x as the risk of a mix, and positive definite means every mix has positive risk](Cards/03-Algebra/07-Eigenvalues%20and%20Symmetric%20Matrices/05-quadratic-forms-and-positive-definite.md)
6. [The singular value decomposition: every matrix is rotate, stretch the axes, rotate, and the stretch factors are the singular values](Cards/03-Algebra/07-Eigenvalues%20and%20Symmetric%20Matrices/06-singular-value-decomposition.md)

<a name="w03-s08"></a>

### 08 · Groups · 8 cards

*The four group rules and the first examples, subgroups and cyclic groups with the order of an element, permutations and the symmetric group, cosets and Lagrange's theorem with Euler's theorem as the payoff, homomorphisms and isomorphisms, normal subgroups and quotient groups, direct products, group actions and counting orbits. Introduces group notation in words on the first card; leans on wing 02 clock arithmetic throughout.*

1. [Groups: one operation, four rules, and the same rules behind clocks, shuffles and symmetries](Cards/03-Algebra/08-Groups/01-groups.md)
2. [Subgroups and cyclic groups: a group inside a group, and the groups one element generates by repeating itself](Cards/03-Algebra/08-Groups/02-subgroups-and-cyclic-groups.md)
3. [Permutations: shuffles compose, every shuffle can be undone, and the n! shuffles of n cards form the symmetric group](Cards/03-Algebra/08-Groups/03-permutations-and-the-symmetric-group.md)
4. [Cosets and Lagrange's theorem: a subgroup slices the group into equal blocks, so its size divides the group's size](Cards/03-Algebra/08-Groups/04-cosets-and-lagranges-theorem.md)
5. [Homomorphisms and isomorphisms: maps that respect the operation, and when two groups are the same group in different clothes](Cards/03-Algebra/08-Groups/05-homomorphisms-and-isomorphisms.md)
6. [Normal subgroups and quotient groups: collapsing a group by a subgroup, and Z mod n is the model](Cards/03-Algebra/08-Groups/06-normal-subgroups-and-quotient-groups.md)
7. [Direct products: run two groups side by side, and when two clocks make one bigger clock](Cards/03-Algebra/08-Groups/07-direct-products.md)
8. [Group actions: a group moving the members of a set, with orbit size times stabiliser size equal to the group's size](Cards/03-Algebra/08-Groups/08-group-actions-and-counting.md)

<a name="w03-s09"></a>

### 09 · Rings and Fields · 5 cards

*Rings with two operations and the clock as the model, zero divisors and units, fields where division always works, polynomials as a ring that behaves like the integers, ideals and quotient rings with Z mod n as the model, finite fields including the four-element field. Rests on shelf 7 and wing 02 clock arithmetic.*

1. [Rings: add, subtract and multiply, but not always divide, and the clock where 3 times 4 is zero](Cards/03-Algebra/09-Rings%20and%20Fields/01-rings.md)
2. [Fields: a ring where every nonzero thing has a reciprocal, so every linear equation has exactly one answer](Cards/03-Algebra/09-Rings%20and%20Fields/02-fields.md)
3. [Polynomials behave like integers: division with remainder, Euclid's algorithm and unique factorisation all work again](Cards/03-Algebra/09-Rings%20and%20Fields/03-polynomials-behave-like-integers.md)
4. [Ideals and quotient rings: the multiples of n are the model, collapsing by them gives Z mod n, and every ring map has one as its kernel](Cards/03-Algebra/09-Rings%20and%20Fields/04-ideals-and-quotient-rings.md)
5. [Finite fields: a clock with a prime number of hours is a field, and a four-hour field exists if you build it from a polynomial](Cards/03-Algebra/09-Rings%20and%20Fields/05-finite-fields.md)

<a name="w03-s10"></a>

### 10 · For the Curious · 5 cards

*Detours with no later dependents: the fundamental theorem of algebra with i glossed, why the quintic has no formula, Gaussian integers and sums of two squares, Pell's equation and root 2, Boolean algebra and lattices. Complex arithmetic proper is wing 06; Galois theory proper is held.*

1. [The fundamental theorem of algebra: allow one new number whose square is -1 and every degree-n polynomial has exactly n roots](Cards/03-Algebra/10-For%20the%20Curious/01-fundamental-theorem-of-algebra.md)
2. [Why there is no quintic formula: degrees 2, 3 and 4 have one, degree 5 provably cannot, because the roots' symmetries are too tangled](Cards/03-Algebra/10-For%20the%20Curious/02-why-no-quintic-formula.md)
3. [Gaussian integers: whole numbers a + bi, where 5 is no longer prime, and which numbers are a sum of two squares](Cards/03-Algebra/10-For%20the%20Curious/03-gaussian-integers-and-sums-of-two-squares.md)
4. [Pell's equation: the whole-number solutions of x^2 - 2y^2 = 1 never run out, and each one is a better fraction for root 2](Cards/03-Algebra/10-For%20the%20Curious/04-pell-equation-and-root-two.md)
5. [Boolean algebra: true/false, sets and switches obey the same rules, and the power set drawn as a cube is a lattice](Cards/03-Algebra/10-For%20the%20Curious/05-boolean-algebra-and-lattices.md)

## 04 · Combinatorics and graphs

Count any finite arrangement (orderings, selections, distributions, partitions) by formula and by brute-force listing, and turn a step rule or a counting problem into a recurrence or a generating function and read the answer off. Model a network as a graph and compute or prove the standard facts: degrees, paths and connectivity, trees and cheapest routes, Euler and Hamilton tours, planarity, colourings, matchings and flows, with Ramsey and extremal results in outline.

### 01 · Counting Principles · 6 cards

*Sum and product rules with dependent choices, strings with repetition and the power set, factorials, ordered picks, n choose k, and counting the complement. Needs only wing 01 arithmetic, sets and functions.*

1. [The rules of sum and product: add the options when they cannot overlap, multiply when they come in stages](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/01-Counting%20Principles/01-rules-of-sum-and-product.md)

4. [Ordered picks: choosing k of n in order is the falling factorial n(n-1)...(n-k+1)](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/01-Counting%20Principles/04-ordered-picks.md)
5. [Combinations, n choose k: unordered picks are ordered picks divided by k!, the workhorse of counting](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/01-Counting%20Principles/05-n-choose-k.md)
6. [Counting the complement: when 'at least one' is hard, count 'none' and subtract from everything](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/01-Counting%20Principles/06-complementary-counting.md)

### 02 · Repeats, Groups and Double Counting · 5 cards

*Arrangements with repeated items, distributing identical items (stars and bars, compositions), splitting into named and unnamed groups, circular arrangements, and proving a count by bijection or by counting twice.*

2. [Stars and bars: identical items into labelled boxes, counted by placing dividers](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/02-Repeats%2C%20Groups%20and%20Double%20Counting/02-stars-and-bars.md)
3. [Splitting into groups: the multinomial coefficient for named piles, and divide by k! when the piles are not named](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/02-Repeats%2C%20Groups%20and%20Double%20Counting/03-splitting-into-groups.md)

5. [Bijections and double counting: match two collections one-to-one, or count one collection two ways, and the numbers must agree](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/02-Repeats%2C%20Groups%20and%20Double%20Counting/05-bijection-and-double-counting.md)

### 03 · Binomial Coefficients and Identities · 7 cards

*Pascal's rule and triangle, the binomial theorem with the multinomial version, Vandermonde, hockey stick, the committee-chair (absorption) identity, alternating sums and binomial inversion, the central binomial coefficient and crude bounds.*

1. [Pascal's rule: each entry is the sum of the two above it, so the whole triangle builds itself and each row sums to 2^n](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/03-Binomial%20Coefficients%20and%20Identities/01-pascals-rule-and-the-triangle.md)
2. [The binomial theorem: (a + b)^n expands with choice counts as coefficients, and so does (a + b + c)^n](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/03-Binomial%20Coefficients%20and%20Identities/02-binomial-theorem.md)
3. [Vandermonde's identity: choosing from two merged groups splits by how many come from each](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/03-Binomial%20Coefficients%20and%20Identities/03-vandermonde-identity.md)
4. [The hockey stick: adding down a diagonal of the triangle lands one step below and right](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/03-Binomial%20Coefficients%20and%20Identities/04-hockey-stick-identity.md)
5. [Committee and chair: k C(n,k) = n C(n-1,k-1), so the weighted row sum is n 2^(n-1)](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/03-Binomial%20Coefficients%20and%20Identities/05-committee-chair-identity.md)
6. [Alternating sums: a row added with alternating signs cancels to zero, which lets you undo a binomial sum](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/03-Binomial%20Coefficients%20and%20Identities/06-alternating-sums-and-binomial-inversion.md)
7. [The middle of the row: C(2n,n) is the biggest entry, and cheap bounds size any choice count without computing it](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/03-Binomial%20Coefficients%20and%20Identities/07-central-binomial-and-bounds.md)

*The n-set inclusion-exclusion formula, derangements, counting onto functions, stopping the sieve early (union bound and Bonferroni), and the pigeonhole principle extended beyond wing 01.*

1. [Inclusion-exclusion for any number of sets: add, subtract the pairs, add the triples, and every element ends up counted once](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/04-Inclusion-Exclusion%20and%20Pigeonhole/01-inclusion-exclusion-for-n-sets.md)
2. [Derangements: shuffles where nothing lands in its own place, counted by the sieve](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/04-Inclusion-Exclusion%20and%20Pigeonhole/02-derangements.md)
3. [Onto functions: hand out every item so nobody is left empty-handed, counted by the sieve](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/04-Inclusion-Exclusion%20and%20Pigeonhole/03-counting-surjections.md)
4. [Stopping the sieve early: the first term over-counts, two terms under-count, and both are guaranteed bounds](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/04-Inclusion-Exclusion%20and%20Pigeonhole/04-union-bound-and-bonferroni.md)
5. [Pigeonhole, extended: n items in k boxes force a box with at least n/k rounded up, and choosing the boxes is the whole trick](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/04-Inclusion-Exclusion%20and%20Pigeonhole/05-pigeonhole-extended.md)

### 05 · Recurrences · 7 cards

*Step rules: Fibonacci and tilings, finite differences and telescoping sums, first-order affine recurrences and loan schedules, the characteristic equation and Binet, driving terms, recurrences as matrix powers, and divide-and-conquer recurrences with the master theorem.*

4. [The characteristic equation: try r^n, solve a quadratic, and any two-term step rule becomes a formula](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/05-Recurrences/04-characteristic-equation-and-binet.md)
5. [Recurrences with a driving term: guess a particular solution of the same shape, add the homogeneous part, fit the seeds](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/05-Recurrences/05-nonhomogeneous-recurrences.md)

### 08 · Partitions · 6 cards

*Integer partitions and Ferrers diagrams, Euler's product, set partitions and Bell numbers, Stirling numbers of the second kind, permutations counted by cycles, and the twelvefold way as the wing's counting map.*

1. [Integer partitions: a number as a sum of whole parts with order ignored, drawn as rows of dots](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/08-Partitions/01-integer-partitions.md)
2. [Euler's product: one geometric factor per part size, and the coefficients count partitions](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/08-Partitions/02-partitions-generating-function.md)
3. [Set partitions and Bell numbers: splitting distinct people into unnamed teams of any sizes](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/08-Partitions/03-set-partitions-and-bell-numbers.md)
4. [Stirling numbers of the second kind: distinct items into exactly k unnamed non-empty groups](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/08-Partitions/04-stirling-numbers-second-kind.md)
5. [Counting shuffles by their loops: Stirling numbers of the first kind, and derangements are the no-short-loop case](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/08-Partitions/05-permutations-by-cycles.md)
6. [The twelvefold way: every 'put n things into k boxes' question in one table](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/08-Partitions/06-twelvefold-way.md)

<a name="w04-s09"></a>

### 09 · Graphs: Dots and Lines · 7 cards

*Graphs and the named families, degrees and the handshaking lemma, walks/paths/cycles with distance, connectivity and breadth-first search, bipartite graphs and odd cycles, directed graphs and DAGs with topological order, and the adjacency matrix.*

1. [Graphs: dots joined by lines, the named families, and when two drawings are the same graph](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/09-Graphs%20-%20Dots%20and%20Lines/01-graphs-vertices-and-edges.md)
2. [Degrees and the handshaking lemma: the degrees add to twice the edges, so the odd-degree vertices come in pairs](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/09-Graphs%20-%20Dots%20and%20Lines/02-degree-and-handshaking.md)
3. [Walks, paths and cycles: a wander, a route with no repeats, and a closed loop, with distance measured in steps](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/09-Graphs%20-%20Dots%20and%20Lines/03-walks-paths-and-cycles.md)
4. [Connected or not: breadth-first search explores ring by ring, finds the pieces, and gives shortest routes when every step costs the same](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/09-Graphs%20-%20Dots%20and%20Lines/04-connectivity-and-breadth-first-search.md)
5. [Bipartite graphs: two sides with no edges inside a side, exactly when there is no odd cycle](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/09-Graphs%20-%20Dots%20and%20Lines/05-bipartite-graphs-and-odd-cycles.md)
6. [Directed graphs: arrows instead of lines, and a graph with no way back can be lined up so every arrow points forward](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/09-Graphs%20-%20Dots%20and%20Lines/06-directed-graphs-and-topological-order.md)
7. [The adjacency matrix: a grid of ones and zeros, and its powers count walks of each length](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/09-Graphs%20-%20Dots%20and%20Lines/07-adjacency-matrix-and-walk-counting.md)

<a name="w04-s10"></a>

### 10 · Trees and Cheapest Routes · 6 cards

*Trees and their characterisations, rooted and binary trees, spanning trees and Cayley's formula, minimum spanning trees by Kruskal and Prim, Dijkstra, and Bellman-Ford with negative cycles as currency arbitrage.*

1. [Trees: connected with no cycles, exactly one fewer edge than vertices, and one route between any two points](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/10-Trees%20and%20Cheapest%20Routes/01-trees.md)
2. [Rooted trees: hang a tree from one vertex and you get parents, children, depth and the shape behind every file system](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/10-Trees%20and%20Cheapest%20Routes/02-rooted-and-binary-trees.md)
3. [Spanning trees: a loop-free skeleton reaching every vertex, and K(n) has n^(n-2) of them](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/10-Trees%20and%20Cheapest%20Routes/03-spanning-trees-and-cayleys-formula.md)
4. [The cheapest skeleton: Kruskal adds the cheapest safe edge, Prim grows from one vertex, and the cut property says both are right](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/10-Trees%20and%20Cheapest%20Routes/04-minimum-spanning-trees.md)
5. [Dijkstra's algorithm: settle the cheapest unsettled point, relax its neighbours, and the cheapest routes appear when no cost is negative](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/10-Trees%20and%20Cheapest%20Routes/05-dijkstra.md)
6. [Bellman-Ford: relax every edge n - 1 times, negative costs allowed, and a loop that still improves is a money machine](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/10-Trees%20and%20Cheapest%20Routes/06-bellman-ford-and-arbitrage.md)

<a name="w04-s11"></a>

### 11 · Tours: Euler and Hamilton · 5 cards

*Euler circuits and trails with Hierholzer's construction, the Chinese postman problem, Hamiltonian cycles with Dirac's condition, the travelling salesman problem in outline, and de Bruijn sequences.*

1. [Euler circuits: a closed route using every edge once exists exactly when the graph is connected and every degree is even](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/11-Tours%20-%20Euler%20and%20Hamilton/01-euler-circuits.md)
2. [The Chinese postman: when no Euler circuit exists, pair up the odd vertices as cheaply as possible and walk those streets twice](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/11-Tours%20-%20Euler%20and%20Hamilton/02-chinese-postman.md)
3. [Hamiltonian cycles: visit every vertex once and return, with no quick test, but enough edges guarantee one](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/11-Tours%20-%20Euler%20and%20Hamilton/03-hamiltonian-cycles.md)
4. [The travelling salesman: the cheapest Hamiltonian cycle, brute force for a few cities, and why nobody has a fast method](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/11-Tours%20-%20Euler%20and%20Hamilton/04-travelling-salesman-in-outline.md)
5. [De Bruijn sequences: an Euler circuit that packs every possible code into one shortest string](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/11-Tours%20-%20Euler%20and%20Hamilton/05-de-bruijn-sequences.md)

<a name="w04-s12"></a>

### 12 · Planarity and Colouring · 6 cards

*Planar graphs and Euler's formula, the edge bound and Kuratowski's two forbidden graphs, vertex colouring and the chromatic number, the chromatic polynomial, the five- and four-colour theorems, and edge colouring with round-robin fixtures.*

1. [Planar graphs: drawn flat with no crossings, and vertices minus edges plus faces is always 2](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/12-Planarity%20and%20Colouring/01-planar-graphs-and-eulers-formula.md)
2. [Why some graphs cannot be drawn flat: at most 3V - 6 edges, so K(5) and the three-utilities graph fail, and Kuratowski says those two are the only obstacles](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/12-Planarity%20and%20Colouring/02-edge-bound-and-kuratowski.md)
3. [Colouring: give joined vertices different colours, and the fewest colours needed is the size of the timetable](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/12-Planarity%20and%20Colouring/03-vertex-colouring-and-chromatic-number.md)
4. [The chromatic polynomial: count the colourings with q colours, by deleting an edge and contracting it](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/12-Planarity%20and%20Colouring/04-chromatic-polynomial.md)
5. [Colouring maps: five colours always suffice by a short argument, four by a famous long one](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/12-Planarity%20and%20Colouring/05-five-and-four-colour-theorems.md)
6. [Edge colouring: colour the edges so no two at a vertex match, and a league fixture list is exactly this](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/12-Planarity%20and%20Colouring/06-edge-colouring-and-round-robin.md)

<a name="w04-s13"></a>

### 13 · Matchings and Flows · 7 cards

*Matchings and augmenting paths, Hall's theorem, Konig's theorem and vertex covers, stable matching by Gale-Shapley, flow networks with Ford-Fulkerson, the max-flow min-cut theorem, and connectivity with Menger's theorem.*

1. [Matchings: pair up vertices with no one used twice, and a matching is largest exactly when no alternating route can improve it](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/13-Matchings%20and%20Flows/01-matchings-and-augmenting-paths.md)
2. [Hall's theorem: everyone on the left can be matched exactly when no group of them shares too few options](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/13-Matchings%20and%20Flows/02-halls-marriage-theorem.md)
3. [Konig's theorem: in a two-sided graph the largest matching equals the smallest set of vertices touching every edge](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/13-Matchings%20and%20Flows/03-konigs-theorem-and-vertex-cover.md)
4. [Stable matching: pair two sides by preference so no two would both rather swap, and the proposers get their best stable deal](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/13-Matchings%20and%20Flows/04-stable-matching-gale-shapley.md)
5. [Flows: pipes with capacities from a source to a sink, and pushing along leftover routes, including undoing an earlier choice](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/13-Matchings%20and%20Flows/05-flow-networks-and-ford-fulkerson.md)
6. [Max-flow min-cut: the most you can push equals the cheapest way to sever the network, and matching is a flow](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/13-Matchings%20and%20Flows/06-max-flow-min-cut.md)
7. [How many cuts break a network: bridges, cut vertices, and Menger's theorem that separate routes equal the blocks needed](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/13-Matchings%20and%20Flows/07-connectivity-and-mengers-theorem.md)

<a name="w04-s14"></a>

### 14 · Ramsey and Extremal, in Outline · 5 cards

*Friends and strangers, Ramsey numbers and the theorem in outline, Erdos's counting lower bound as the first probabilistic-method argument, Erdos-Szekeres with Dilworth folded, and Mantel and Turan on triangle-free and clique-free graphs.*

1. [Friends and strangers: among any six people, three all know each other or three are all strangers, and five is not enough](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/14-Ramsey%20and%20Extremal%2C%20in%20Outline/01-friends-and-strangers.md)
2. [Ramsey numbers: the size at which a pattern is forced, known exactly for only a handful of cases](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/14-Ramsey%20and%20Extremal%2C%20in%20Outline/02-ramsey-numbers.md)
3. [Erdos's counting trick: if the bad colourings are fewer than all colourings, a good one exists, so R(k,k) grows at least like 2^(k/2)](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/14-Ramsey%20and%20Extremal%2C%20in%20Outline/03-probabilistic-method-by-counting.md)
4. [Erdos-Szekeres: any long enough list of numbers has a long rising run or a long falling run, by labelling and pigeonhole](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/14-Ramsey%20and%20Extremal%2C%20in%20Outline/04-erdos-szekeres.md)
5. [Mantel and Turan: more than n^2/4 edges force a triangle, and the balanced multipartite graph is the most you can have without a clique](Cards/04-Combinatorics%20and%20graphs/14-Ramsey%20and%20Extremal%2C%20in%20Outline/05-mantel-and-turan.md)

## 05 · Geometry and trig

Measure and reason about shapes in the plane and in space: angles, lengths, areas and volumes by formula and by argument, any triangle from three pieces of data, points and curves by coordinates in three systems, and vectors in space. The wing ends where the flat plane stops being the only option, so that later wings can talk about spheres, curved space and points on a cubic curve.

<a name="w07"></a>

## 07 · Complex analysis

Compute with complex numbers, decide when a function is holomorphic, integrate round loops with residues, evaluate real integrals and count zeros by contour, map regions conformally and solve boundary problems on them, and use Fourier and Laplace transforms in outline. Meet the gamma and zeta functions, analytic continuation and the functional equation, and read the route to the Riemann hypothesis that wings 20, 21 and 25 climb.

9 shelves · **60 of 60 cards ready to read** · [open the folder](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis)

| Shelf | Cards |
| --- | ---: |
| [01 · Complex Numbers and the Plane](#w07-s01) | 7 |
| [02 · Holomorphic Functions](#w07-s02) | 5 |
| [03 · Contour Integrals and Cauchy's Theorem](#w07-s03) | 7 |
| [04 · Taylor Series, Zeros and Rigidity](#w07-s04) | 5 |
| [05 · Laurent Series, Singularities and Residues](#w07-s05) | 5 |
| [06 · Real Integrals and Counting Zeros](#w07-s06) | 7 |
| [07 · Conformal Maps and Harmonic Functions](#w07-s07) | 8 |
| [08 · Transforms in Outline](#w07-s08) | 7 |
| [09 · Special Functions and the Zeta Function](#w07-s09) | 9 |

<a name="w07-s01"></a>

### 01 · Complex Numbers and the Plane · 7 cards

*a + bi and the plane, conjugate and modulus, polar form and the argument, Euler's formula, powers and roots, limits and regions, complex vectors and matrices (wing 03's hand-off). Needs wing 01 arithmetic, wing 03 quadratics and the FTA curious card, wing 05 radians and angle-sum identities, wing 06 Taylor series for Euler.*

1. [Complex numbers: one new number i with i squared = -1, and every pair of coordinates becomes a number you can multiply](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/01-Complex%20Numbers%20and%20the%20Plane/01-complex-numbers.md)
2. [Conjugate and modulus: flip across the axis, measure the distance, and division falls out](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/01-Complex%20Numbers%20and%20the%20Plane/02-conjugate-and-modulus.md)
3. [Polar form: a length and an angle, so multiplying means turn and stretch](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/01-Complex%20Numbers%20and%20the%20Plane/03-polar-form-and-argument.md)
4. [Euler's formula: e to the i-theta is the point at angle theta on the unit circle, so waves and turns are exponentials](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/01-Complex%20Numbers%20and%20the%20Plane/04-eulers-formula.md)
5. [Powers and roots: de Moivre multiplies the angle, so the n-th roots sit evenly round a circle](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/01-Complex%20Numbers%20and%20the%20Plane/05-powers-roots-and-roots-of-unity.md)
6. [Limits and regions in the plane: a complex limit is two real limits, plus the words disc, boundary and domain](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/01-Complex%20Numbers%20and%20the%20Plane/06-complex-limits-series-and-regions.md)
7. [Complex vectors and matrices: the dot product grows a conjugate, and a rotation's eigenvalues come home](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/01-Complex%20Numbers%20and%20the%20Plane/07-complex-vectors-and-matrices.md)

<a name="w07-s02"></a>

### 02 · Holomorphic Functions · 5 cards

*The complex derivative and Cauchy-Riemann, power series in the plane, exp, sine, cosine and their hyperbolic twins, the logarithm and its branches, branch cuts and complex powers. Needs wing 06 derivatives, partial derivatives and power-series convergence.*

1. [The complex derivative: one limit from every direction, and the Cauchy-Riemann equations that make it possible](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/02-Holomorphic%20Functions/01-complex-derivative-and-cauchy-riemann.md)
2. [Power series in the plane: a disc of convergence, and the invisible poles that set its radius](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/02-Holomorphic%20Functions/02-complex-power-series.md)
3. [The elementary functions: exp, sine and cosine for complex inputs, and the hyperbolic twins are the same functions turned a quarter](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/02-Holomorphic%20Functions/03-exponential-sine-and-cosine-in-the-plane.md)
4. [The complex logarithm: undo e^z, and find infinitely many answers a floor apart](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/02-Holomorphic%20Functions/04-complex-logarithm.md)
5. [Branch cuts and complex powers: z to the a is e to the a log z, it depends on the branch, and software picks one for you](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/02-Holomorphic%20Functions/05-branch-cuts-and-complex-powers.md)

<a name="w07-s03"></a>

### 03 · Contour Integrals and Cauchy's Theorem · 7 cards

*Integrals along paths and the ML bound, antiderivatives and path independence, Cauchy's theorem, deforming loops and winding numbers, Cauchy's integral formula, derivatives from the boundary with Cauchy's estimates, Liouville and the fundamental theorem of algebra. Needs wing 06 line integrals and Green's theorem.*

1. [Contour integrals: add up f(z) dz along a path, and the loop round the origin that gives 2 pi i](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/03-Contour%20Integrals%20and%20Cauchy%27s%20Theorem/01-contour-integrals.md)
2. [Antiderivatives: when f = F' the path does not matter, and the one function that has none](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/03-Contour%20Integrals%20and%20Cauchy%27s%20Theorem/02-antiderivatives-and-path-independence.md)
3. [Cauchy's theorem: a holomorphic function integrates to zero round any loop with no hole inside](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/03-Contour%20Integrals%20and%20Cauchy%27s%20Theorem/03-cauchys-theorem.md)
4. [Deforming a loop: slide it anywhere without crossing a singularity, and count how many times it winds](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/03-Contour%20Integrals%20and%20Cauchy%27s%20Theorem/04-deforming-contours-and-winding-numbers.md)
5. [Cauchy's integral formula: the values on a loop fix every value inside](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/03-Contour%20Integrals%20and%20Cauchy%27s%20Theorem/05-cauchys-integral-formula.md)
6. [Derivatives from the boundary: differentiate under the integral, so holomorphic once means holomorphic forever, with a size limit on every derivative](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/03-Contour%20Integrals%20and%20Cauchy%27s%20Theorem/06-derivatives-from-the-boundary.md)
7. [Liouville's theorem: a bounded function holomorphic everywhere is constant, and so every polynomial has a root](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/03-Contour%20Integrals%20and%20Cauchy%27s%20Theorem/07-liouville-and-the-fundamental-theorem-of-algebra.md)

<a name="w07-s04"></a>

### 04 · Taylor Series, Zeros and Rigidity · 5 cards

*Taylor series with radius = distance to the nearest singularity, uniform limits of holomorphic functions, isolated zeros and the identity theorem, analytic continuation, the maximum modulus principle. Rests on shelf 3.*

1. [Taylor series in the plane: a holomorphic function equals its power series out to the nearest singularity](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/04-Taylor%20Series%2C%20Zeros%20and%20Rigidity/01-taylor-series-in-the-plane.md)
2. [Limits of holomorphic functions: if the approximations converge evenly, the limit is holomorphic and its derivatives follow](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/04-Taylor%20Series%2C%20Zeros%20and%20Rigidity/02-uniform-limits-of-holomorphic-functions.md)
3. [Zeros and the identity theorem: zeros come singly, so a function known on a tiny stretch is known everywhere](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/04-Taylor%20Series%2C%20Zeros%20and%20Rigidity/03-zeros-and-the-identity-theorem.md)
4. [Analytic continuation: push a function past the edge of its formula, and the extension is the only one possible](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/04-Taylor%20Series%2C%20Zeros%20and%20Rigidity/04-analytic-continuation.md)
5. [The maximum modulus principle: |f| has no interior peak, so its largest value sits on the boundary](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/04-Taylor%20Series%2C%20Zeros%20and%20Rigidity/05-maximum-modulus-principle.md)

<a name="w07-s05"></a>

### 05 · Laurent Series, Singularities and Residues · 5 cards

*Laurent series in a ring, the three kinds of isolated singularity, rational functions as sums of principal parts, residues and how to compute them, the residue theorem. Rests on shelves 3 and 4.*

1. [Laurent series: allow negative powers, and a function with a hole expands in a ring](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/05-Laurent%20Series%2C%20Singularities%20and%20Residues/01-laurent-series.md)
2. [Isolated singularities: removable, a pole or essential, and the negative powers tell you which](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/05-Laurent%20Series%2C%20Singularities%20and%20Residues/02-classifying-singularities.md)
3. [Rational functions: a ratio of polynomials is the sum of its principal parts, one per pole](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/05-Laurent%20Series%2C%20Singularities%20and%20Residues/03-rational-functions-and-partial-fractions.md)
4. [Residues: the one coefficient that survives a loop, and three ways to compute it](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/05-Laurent%20Series%2C%20Singularities%20and%20Residues/04-residues.md)
5. [The residue theorem: a loop integral is 2 pi i times the sum of the residues inside](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/05-Laurent%20Series%2C%20Singularities%20and%20Residues/05-the-residue-theorem.md)

<a name="w07-s06"></a>

### 06 · Real Integrals and Counting Zeros · 7 cards

*Semicircle contours for rational integrals, the unit-circle substitution for trig integrals, Jordan's lemma, indented contours and principal values, keyhole contours round a branch cut, the argument principle, Rouche's theorem. Rests on shelf 5 and the branch-cut card.*

1. [The semicircle contour: close the real line with a big arc, let the arc vanish, and read the integral off the poles above](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/06-Real%20Integrals%20and%20Counting%20Zeros/01-semicircle-contours.md)
2. [Integrals round a full turn: set z = e to the i-theta and the trig integral becomes a residue on the unit circle](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/06-Real%20Integrals%20and%20Counting%20Zeros/02-trigonometric-integrals-on-the-unit-circle.md)
3. [Jordan's lemma: with an e to the iax factor the big arc still vanishes, so cosine integrals fall to residues](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/06-Real%20Integrals%20and%20Counting%20Zeros/03-oscillatory-integrals-and-jordans-lemma.md)
4. [Poles on the path: dent the contour round them and collect half a residue, and the principal value is what is left](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/06-Real%20Integrals%20and%20Counting%20Zeros/04-indented-contours-and-principal-values.md)
5. [The keyhole contour: wrap a branch cut, and the two banks disagree by exactly the factor that solves the integral](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/06-Real%20Integrals%20and%20Counting%20Zeros/05-keyhole-contours.md)
6. [The argument principle: walk the boundary and watch the output spin; the turns count zeros minus poles inside](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/06-Real%20Integrals%20and%20Counting%20Zeros/06-the-argument-principle.md)
7. [Rouche's theorem: a dog on a short lead circles the post as often as its owner, so small changes never change the root count](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/06-Real%20Integrals%20and%20Counting%20Zeros/07-rouches-theorem.md)

### 07 · Conformal Maps and Harmonic Functions · 8 cards

*Local rotate-and-stretch, Mobius maps and the point at infinity, the standard maps and how to compose them, harmonic functions and conjugates, mean value and maximum principle, the Poisson formula, transporting boundary problems by mapping, the Riemann mapping theorem and Schwarz-Christoffel in outline. Rests on shelves 2 and 3.*

1. [Conformal maps: where f' is not zero a holomorphic function turns and stretches every tiny shape, so angles survive](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/07-Conformal%20Maps%20and%20Harmonic%20Functions/01-conformal-maps.md)
2. [Mobius transformations: (az + b)/(cz + d) sends circles and lines to circles and lines, once infinity counts as a point](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/07-Conformal%20Maps%20and%20Harmonic%20Functions/02-mobius-transformations-and-the-point-at-infinity.md)
3. [The standard maps: z squared, e to the z, log and z to the a turn wedges, strips and half planes into one another, and you chain them to reach the shape you need](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/07-Conformal%20Maps%20and%20Harmonic%20Functions/03-standard-maps-and-composing-them.md)
4. [Harmonic functions: the real and imaginary parts of a holomorphic function both solve Laplace's equation, and their level curves cross at right angles](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/07-Conformal%20Maps%20and%20Harmonic%20Functions/04-harmonic-functions-and-conjugates.md)
5. [Mean value and maximum principle: a harmonic function at a centre is the average round the circle, so its extremes sit on the boundary and the boundary fixes everything](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/07-Conformal%20Maps%20and%20Harmonic%20Functions/05-mean-value-and-maximum-principle-for-harmonic-functions.md)
6. [The Poisson formula: fill a disc from its rim by weighting each boundary value by how close it is](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/07-Conformal%20Maps%20and%20Harmonic%20Functions/06-poisson-integral-formula.md)
7. [Solving by mapping: a harmonic function stays harmonic under a conformal map, so solve on the disc or half plane and carry the answer back](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/07-Conformal%20Maps%20and%20Harmonic%20Functions/07-solving-boundary-problems-by-mapping.md)
8. [The Riemann mapping theorem: any region without holes, short of the whole plane, is a disc in disguise, and Schwarz-Christoffel writes the map for polygons](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/07-Conformal%20Maps%20and%20Harmonic%20Functions/08-riemann-mapping-theorem-and-schwarz-christoffel.md)

<a name="w07-s08"></a>

### 08 · Transforms in Outline · 7 cards

*Fourier series in complex form, the discrete Fourier transform through roots of unity, the Fourier transform, convolution, the Laplace transform, strips of convergence and sliding the line, inversion by residues. Outline: existence and inversion theorems are stated here and proved in wing 20; wing 08 uses Laplace to solve equations; wings 09 and 12 apply the strip and damping to characteristic functions and pricing.*

1. [Fourier series: any repeating signal is a sum of spinning arrows e to the inx, and each coefficient is an average](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/08-Transforms%20in%20Outline/01-fourier-series-in-complex-form.md)
2. [The discrete Fourier transform: N samples become N frequencies through the N-th roots of unity, and the matrix is unitary](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/08-Transforms%20in%20Outline/02-discrete-fourier-transform.md)
3. [The Fourier transform: a continuous dial of frequencies, and the Gaussian is its own transform](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/08-Transforms%20in%20Outline/03-fourier-transform.md)
4. [Convolution: smear one signal with another, and under the transform the smear becomes a multiplication](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/08-Transforms%20in%20Outline/04-convolution-theorem.md)
5. [The Laplace transform: multiply by e to the minus st and integrate from zero, and derivatives become multiplication by s](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/08-Transforms%20in%20Outline/05-laplace-transform.md)
6. [Where a transform lives: a strip or half plane of convergence, and inside it the line of integration slides without changing the answer](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/08-Transforms%20in%20Outline/06-strips-of-convergence-and-shifting-the-line.md)
7. [Inverting a Laplace transform: integrate up a vertical line, close it to the left, and every pole hands back an exponential](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/08-Transforms%20in%20Outline/07-inverse-laplace-by-residues.md)

<a name="w07-s09"></a>

### 09 · Special Functions and the Zeta Function · 9 cards

*Infinite products, the gamma and beta functions, Stirling's formula, the zeta function and the Euler product, Dirichlet series and Mobius inversion (wing 02's hand-off), the Mellin transform, zeta continued and its functional equation, the zeros and the primes. Rests on shelves 4, 6 and 8 and wing 02 unique factorisation.*

1. [Infinite products: a product converges exactly when the sum of its small parts does, and sine is a product over its zeros](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/09-Special%20Functions%20and%20the%20Zeta%20Function/01-infinite-products.md)
2. [The gamma function: the factorial for every number, defined by an integral and stretched to the whole plane by its own recurrence](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/09-Special%20Functions%20and%20the%20Zeta%20Function/02-gamma-function.md)
3. [The beta function: an integral over 0 to 1 built from two gammas, the shape behind every bump between 0 and 1](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/09-Special%20Functions%20and%20the%20Zeta%20Function/03-beta-function.md)
4. [Stirling's formula: n! is about sqrt(2 pi n) times (n/e) to the n, from the peak of the gamma integral](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/09-Special%20Functions%20and%20the%20Zeta%20Function/04-stirlings-formula.md)
5. [The zeta function: the sum of 1 over n to the s for complex s, holomorphic past Re s = 1, and equal to a product over the primes](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/09-Special%20Functions%20and%20the%20Zeta%20Function/05-zeta-function-and-euler-product.md)
6. [Dirichlet series: multiply two of them and the coefficients convolve over divisors, so 1 over zeta is a series with the Mobius signs](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/09-Special%20Functions%20and%20the%20Zeta%20Function/06-dirichlet-series-and-mobius-inversion.md)
7. [The Mellin transform: a Laplace transform on a log scale, whose first two examples are gamma and zeta](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/09-Special%20Functions%20and%20the%20Zeta%20Function/07-mellin-transform.md)
8. [Continuing zeta: past s = 1 with an alternating series, a pole at 1, and a mirror that reflects s to 1 - s](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/09-Special%20Functions%20and%20the%20Zeta%20Function/08-continuing-zeta-and-the-functional-equation.md)
9. [Zeta's zeros and the primes: the zeros are the frequencies hidden in the prime staircase, and the Riemann hypothesis says where they lie](Cards/07-Complex%20analysis/09-Special%20Functions%20and%20the%20Zeta%20Function/09-zeros-of-zeta-and-the-primes.md)

<a name="w08"></a>

## 08 · Differential equations and dynamics

Write down a rate law for a real situation, solve it by hand where a formula exists, step it on a computer where none does, and say whether the answer is trustworthy, stable, oscillating or chaotic. Carry the same toolkit into heat, waves and steady states, into discrete iteration, and into choosing the best path or control, ready for the stochastic and financial versions in wings 11 and 12.

12 shelves · **93 of 93 cards ready to read** · [open the folder](Cards/08-Differential%20equations%20and%20dynamics)

| Shelf | Cards |
| --- | ---: |
| [01 · Rate Equations](#w08-s01) | 9 |
| [02 · Existence, Uniqueness and Sensitivity](#w08-s02) | 5 |
| [03 · Oscillators: Second-Order Linear Equations](#w08-s03) | 9 |
| [04 · Systems and the Matrix Exponential](#w08-s04) | 7 |
| [05 · Numerical Evolution](#w08-s05) | 7 |
| [06 · Nonlinear Dynamics in the Plane](#w08-s06) | 10 |
