<a name="w17"></a>

## 17 · Topology

Work in a metric or an abstract topological space, and prove the compactness and connectedness facts that every analysis wing has been leaning on. Classify a closed surface by hand, compute a fundamental group and a homology group on a concrete example, and run persistent homology on real data while saying honestly what it is worth.

6 shelves · **0 of 51 cards ready to read** · all planned

| Shelf | Cards |
| --- | ---: |
| [01 · Metric Spaces](#w17-s01) | 10 |
| [02 · Topological Spaces](#w17-s02) | 10 |
| [03 · Surfaces and Manifolds](#w17-s03) | 8 |
| [04 · Homotopy](#w17-s04) | 8 |
| [05 · Homology](#w17-s05) | 8 |
| [06 · Topological Data Analysis](#w17-s06) | 7 |

<a name="w17-s01"></a>

### 01 · Metric Spaces · 10 cards

*The three distance rules, the vocabulary they generate, and the four theorems that need completeness*

1. Metric space: the three rules that make a distance mean something
2. The distance menu: straight-line, taxicab, worst-case, and distance between whole shapes
3. Open balls, open sets and closed sets: the vocabulary a distance hands you
4. Limits and continuity with distances: epsilon and delta in any metric space
5. Complete space: bunched-up sequences land somewhere, and how to fill in the gaps
6. Compactness three ways: finite covers, convergent subsequences, closed and bounded
7. Uniform continuity: one tolerance for the whole space, and free on a compact set
8. Banach's fixed point theorem: a shrinking map on a complete space has one fixed point
9. Arzela-Ascoli: when a whole family of functions has a convergent subsequence
10. Baire category theorem: a complete space is never a countable pile of thin sets

<a name="w17-s02"></a>

### 02 · Topological Spaces · 10 cards

*Nearness with no distances at all, the three standard constructions, and the separation and compactness theorems*

1. Topological space: you name the open sets, and a basis generates them
2. Closure, interior and boundary: the solid inside, the edge, and everything a set touches
3. Continuity by pre-images, and homeomorphism: when two shapes are the same for topology
4. Subspace, product and quotient: cutting a piece out, pairing up, gluing points together
5. Hausdorff and its ladder: how far apart open sets can pry two points
6. Compactness with no distances, and Tychonoff: any product of compact spaces is compact
7. Connected and path-connected: one piece, and one piece you can walk across
8. What compactness and connectedness buy: attained maxima, crossed values, free inverses
9. The test spaces: discrete, indiscrete, cofinite, the Cantor set, and what each one breaks
10. Urysohn and Tietze: a dial between closed sets, and when a space is secretly metric

<a name="w17-s03"></a>

### 03 · Surfaces and Manifolds · 8 cards

*Spaces that look flat up close, the complete list of closed surfaces, and the invariants that separate them*

1. Topological manifold: a space that looks like ordinary flat space up close
2. Closed surfaces classified: a sphere with handles or crosscaps, and nothing else
3. Orientability: whether a surface has two sides, and the famous ones that do not
4. Euler characteristic: vertices minus edges plus faces, the number bending cannot change
5. Jordan curve theorem: a simple closed loop cuts the plane into an inside and an outside
6. Knots and links: closed loops in space, their flat diagrams, and what an invariant is
7. Reidemeister moves and the three-colour test: proof that the trefoil cannot be untied
8. Knot polynomials in outline: one skein rule turns a diagram into a polynomial

<a name="w17-s04"></a>

### 04 · Homotopy · 8 cards

*Deforming maps continuously, the group of loops, and the two fixed-point theorems it pays for*

1. Homotopy: sliding one map into another, and spaces that squash into each other
2. Contractible and simply connected: no holes at all, and every loop can be shrunk
3. The fundamental group: loops at a point, multiplied by running one after the other
4. The circle's fundamental group is the integers: winding number does all the counting
5. Covering spaces: unroll a space so its loops become paths you can follow
6. Van Kampen's theorem: glue two pieces together and glue their loop groups together
7. CW complexes: build a space by gluing cells, then read its invariants off the recipe
8. Brouwer and Borsuk-Ulam: a disc map fixes a point, and antipodes give equal readings

<a name="w17-s05"></a>

### 05 · Homology · 8 cards

*Counting holes with linear algebra: chains, cycles, boundaries, and the sequences that compute them*

1. Simplicial complexes and chains: triangles glued up, with a boundary that squares to zero
2. Homology groups: cycles that are not boundaries, one hole count per dimension
3. Smith normal form: the matrix routine that turns boundary matrices into hole counts
4. Homotopy invariance: spaces you can deform into each other have identical hole counts
5. Cellular homology: hole counts read straight off the gluing recipe, quick by hand
6. Mayer-Vietoris and exact sequences: the holes of a whole from two overlapping pieces
7. Homology of spheres, and degree: how many times one sphere wraps around another
8. Cohomology and the cup product in outline: the dual view, carrying a multiplication

<a name="w17-s06"></a>

### 06 · Topological Data Analysis · 7 cards

*Turning data into shapes at every scale, reading the diagram, and knowing when to believe it*

1. Filtration and persistent homology: watch each hole appear and close as the scale grows
2. Vietoris-Rips, Cech and alpha complexes: three ways to make a shape out of points
3. The persistence algorithm: column reduction pairs every birth with its death
4. Persistence diagram and stability: noise in the data moves the picture only a little
5. Comparing diagrams: bottleneck distance, landscapes, images, and honest error bars
6. Mapper and Reeb graphs: a network summary of a dataset along a chosen lens
7. Persistence in practice: the shape of a time series, and what that shape is worth

## 18 · Functional analysis

Work in infinite-dimensional spaces as steadily as in ordinary space: measure size with a norm, rely on completeness, name the dual, and read an operator through its spectrum. Differentiate kinks and spikes, pose an equation weakly in a Sobolev space, and turn a generator into an evolution that runs forward in time.

<a name="w18-s01"></a>

### 01 · Normed and Banach Spaces · 10 cards

*length and completeness in spaces of sequences and functions; the three Banach theorems; Hahn-Banach and the dual space*

1. Normed and Banach spaces: giving a length to functions so limits cannot escape
2. Sequence spaces: infinite lists of numbers with a size rule
3. Function spaces C and Lp: two ways to size a function, two different worlds
4. Equivalent norms: in finitely many dimensions every length rule agrees
5. Infinite dimensions: the unit ball stops being compact and bases get subtler
6. Hahn-Banach: a measurement on part of a space extends to all of it, no bigger
7. Separation: two convex sets that miss each other can be split by a flat wall
8. Three Banach theorems: completeness turns weak checks into full continuity
9. Dual spaces: the continuous measurements on a space, and what they turn out to be
10. Reflexive spaces: when a space is exactly the dual of its own dual

<a name="w18-s02"></a>

### 02 · Hilbert Spaces · 9 cards

*inner products and angles; projection; orthonormal expansions and Parseval; representation of functionals; kernels; weak limits*

1. Inner products: length and angle in a space of signals
2. Projection: every point has exactly one nearest point in a closed subspace
3. Orthonormal bases: every signal is an infinite sum of perpendicular pieces
4. Completeness of sines and cosines: Fourier series leave nothing behind
5. Orthogonal polynomials: straightening the powers into a perpendicular family
6. Riesz representation: every measurement on a Hilbert space is one fixed vector
7. Lax-Milgram: a bounded energy form that stays away from zero has one solution
8. Reproducing kernels: a space where reading off a value is an inner product
9. Weak convergence: only the measurements must settle, and that buys compactness

<a name="w18-s03"></a>

### 03 · Bounded Operators · 10 cards

*operator norm and continuity; the model operators; adjoints; compactness; spectrum and resolvent; the two spectral theorems*

1. Bounded operators: for a linear map, no blow-up is the same as continuity
2. Three model operators: integrate against a kernel, multiply, shift
3. Adjoints: the mirror operator, and the classes it singles out
4. Plancherel: the Fourier transform is a rigid rotation of the space of signals
5. Compact operators: the maps that still behave like finite matrices
6. Neumann series: a geometric series that inverts an operator near the identity
7. Spectrum: the numbers at which an operator cannot be inverted
8. Compact and symmetric: an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors, eigenvalues dying away
9. Fredholm alternative: either always solvable, or finitely many obstructions
10. Bounded and symmetric: diagonal against a family of projections, not eigenvectors

<a name="w18-s04"></a>

### 04 · Distributions and Sobolev Spaces · 10 cards

*test functions and distributions; delta and jumps; convolution and mollifiers; tempered transforms; weak derivatives and Sobolev estimates*

1. Distributions: objects defined only by what they read on smooth probes
2. The delta: a spike of unit mass, and the derivative of a switch
3. Convolution and mollifiers: smoothing a rough object without moving it
4. Fundamental solution: solve once for a spike, then convolve for any load
5. Tempered distributions: taking the Fourier transform of a hum or a spike
6. Sampling: a row of spikes explains why evenly spaced samples suffice
7. Weak derivatives: differentiating a kink, and the spaces that keep score
8. Weak solutions: asking an equation to hold only against smooth probes
9. Sobolev and Poincare: trading derivative control for size and smoothness
10. Boundary values and compactness: the two Sobolev results existence proofs lean on

<a name="w18-s05"></a>

### 05 · Unbounded Operators and Semigroups · 9 cards

*domains, closedness and self-adjointness; semigroups and their generators; forcing, splitting, and probabilistic evolutions*

1. Unbounded operators: differentiation only works where you let it
2. Self-adjoint extensions: symmetric is not enough, the domain decides
3. Evolution as an equation: rate of change equals an operator times the state
4. Semigroups: a family of operators that composes like elapsed time
5. Hille-Yosida: exactly which operators drive a well-behaved evolution
6. Duhamel: add up each nudge, carried forward by the unforced flow
7. Splitting: alternate two evolutions in tiny steps to get their combination
8. Feller semigroups: the evolution of averages along a random path
9. Two evolutions: heat smooths at once, waves only rotate

<a name="w18-s06"></a>

### 06 · Banach Algebras and Fixed Points · 8 cards

*multiplying in a complete space; the Gelfand picture and C-star algebras; fixed points; extreme points and convex duality*

1. Banach algebras: a complete space you can also multiply in
2. Wiener's lemma: a filter that never vanishes has a well-behaved inverse
3. C-star algebras: an algebra with a mirror operation whose sizes match exactly
4. States: an expectation on an algebra, and the space it builds
5. Schauder: a continuous map of a compact convex set must leave a point unmoved
6. Krein-Milman: a compact convex set is rebuilt from its corners
7. The slope transform: recoding a convex function by its supporting lines
8. Minimax: under convexity the best worst case equals the worst best case

<a name="w19-s01"></a>

### 01 · Classification and Well-Posedness · 9 cards

*What a PDE problem is, the three families, and what it takes for a problem to be well posed*

1. A PDE problem: the equation, the region, and the data that pin it down
2. Linear or not: when you may add two solutions together
3. Boundary conditions: fix the value, fix the flow, or mix the two
4. Elliptic, parabolic, hyperbolic: three families with three behaviours
5. Canonical form: change coordinates until you recognise the equation
6. Well posed: a solution exists, is unique, and does not amplify errors
7. Energy methods: multiply by the solution and integrate
8. Maximum principles: the extreme values sit on the boundary
9. Dependence and influence: which data reaches a point, and how far it spreads

<a name="w19-s02"></a>

### 02 · First-Order Equations and Characteristics · 7 cards

*First-order equations solved along characteristics, the shocks they form, and the value equations of control*

1. Characteristics: curves along which a PDE becomes an ODE
2. Conservation laws: characteristics cross, and a front appears
3. Entropy conditions: choosing the jump the real system would pick
4. The Riemann problem: two constant states meeting at one point
5. Hamilton-Jacobi: a first-order equation for a value or an arrival time
6. The HJB equation: the value of steering a system well
7. Eikonal and traffic: arrival times and jams as first-order equations

<a name="w19-s03"></a>

### 03 · The Heat Equation in Depth · 10 cards

*Diffusion solved by its kernel, its principles, and the pricing equations that are diffusion in disguise*

1. The heat kernel: the bell-shaped answer to a single hot spot
2. Similarity solutions: diffusion looks the same at every scale
3. Duhamel's principle: a forcing term is a stream of fresh starts
4. Diffusion's maximum principle: the extremes sit on the start or the sides
5. Diffusion smooths instantly, and is felt everywhere at once
6. Convection-diffusion: drift carries the quantity while spreading blurs it
7. Black-Scholes is the heat equation after a change of variables
8. Feynman-Kac: a diffusion equation is an average over random paths
9. Fokker-Planck: how the spread of a random quantity moves forward
10. Dupire's equation: reading volatility off a surface of prices

<a name="w19-s04"></a>

### 04 · Laplace, Poisson and Potentials · 10 cards

*Harmonic functions, potentials, Green's functions, and the eigenvalue problem behind every mode*

1. Harmonic functions: the value at a point is the average around it
2. The fundamental solution: the potential of a single point source
3. Poisson's equation: smear the point sources to get the potential
4. Green's function: one poke's response rebuilds the whole solution
5. Method of images: a mirror source satisfies the boundary for free
6. Dirichlet's principle: the steady state stores the least energy
7. The Laplacian in round coordinates, and the modes it splits into
8. Helmholtz: the shapes and frequencies a region can hold
9. Subharmonic functions: trap the solution between under and over shapes
10. Harnack and regularity: harmonic functions cannot spike, and are smooth

<a name="w19-s05"></a>

### 05 · Waves · 8 cards

*Waves by characteristics and closed formulas, their energy, their dispersion and their modes*

1. d'Alembert: a wave splits into a left-mover and a right-mover
2. Wave energy is conserved, and nothing outruns the wave speed
3. Reflection: a clamped end is a mirror placed in the data
4. Kirchhoff's formula: in three dimensions a pulse passes and leaves silence
5. The retarded Green's function: the answer arrives only after the poke
6. Dispersion: when each wavelength travels at its own speed
7. Schrodinger and Klein-Gordon: spreading that never dies away
8. Drumheads: the modes of a round membrane are Bessel patterns

<a name="w19-s06"></a>

### 06 · Weak Solutions and Free Boundaries · 9 cards

*Solutions that need no second derivative, the existence theory for them, and boundaries found as part of the answer*

1. Weak solutions: multiply by a test function and integrate by parts
2. Sobolev spaces: the right home for a solution of limited smoothness
3. Lax-Milgram: one weak solution when the form is bounded and coercive
4. Galerkin: solve the weak form on finitely many chosen shapes
5. Elliptic regularity: the weak solution turns out to be smooth after all
6. Viscosity solutions: test with smooth functions that touch the graph
7. Free boundaries: when the edge of the region is part of the answer
8. The obstacle problem: a membrane that must clear an obstacle
9. An American option: the boundary where holding stops beating exercising

<a name="w19-s07"></a>

### 07 · Nonlinear PDE and Fluids · 9 cards

*The fluid equations, the regularity question, patterns and solitons, and equations with the unknown under an integral*

1. Navier-Stokes: mass and momentum balance for a moving fluid
2. Leray's weak solutions: they exist in three dimensions, but may not be unique
3. The energy inequality, and the gap it leaves in three dimensions
4. Vorticity: the spin form of the equations, and the zero-viscosity limit
5. Reaction-diffusion: spreading plus local growth makes fronts and patterns
6. Solitons: when steepening and spreading balance exactly
7. Integral equations: the unknown sits inside the integral
8. The Fredholm alternative: either exactly one solution, or extra free ones
9. When prices jump: a pricing equation with an integral term

<a name="w20-s01"></a>

### 01 · Fourier Series in Depth · 9 cards

*Coefficients, kernels, and exactly when and in what sense the series equals the function*

1. Fourier coefficients: each wave's share, read off by an integral that ignores the rest
2. Partial sums are the best fit: least squares in function space, and Bessel's inequality
3. Dirichlet and Fejer kernels: a partial sum is the signal smeared by a fixed shape
4. Dirichlet's theorem: where the series equals the signal, and where it averages the jump
5. Convergence in energy: Parseval, completeness, and Riesz-Fischer
6. Smoothness shows in the tail: how fast coefficients shrink, and how many terms you need
7. Differentiating a series term by term: when it is legal, and what integration costs
8. The Gibbs overshoot and the cure: Fejer averaging and half-range series
9. Carleson's theorem in outline: almost everywhere, the series does converge

<a name="w20-s02"></a>

### 02 · The Fourier Transform · 10 cards

*The transform of an integrable and of a square-integrable signal, inversion, energy, distributions, and the limits on locality*

1. The Fourier transform of an absolutely integrable signal, and why it fades at infinity
2. The transform rulebook: shifting, scaling, and turning derivatives into multiplication
3. Two pairs worth memorising: gate and sinc, and the bell curve that transforms to itself
4. Fourier inversion: rebuilding the signal from its spectrum, proved with a bell curve
5. Plancherel: the transform preserves energy, and extends to every square-integrable signal
6. Schwartz space and tempered distributions: transforms of spikes, constants and steps
7. The uncertainty principle: no signal is both brief and narrow in frequency
8. Poisson summation and sampling: samples are enough when nothing wiggles too fast
9. The Hilbert transform: shift every frequency a quarter turn, and read off the envelope
10. Transforms in two and three dimensions: a slice of the spectrum is a shadow of the object

<a name="w20-s03"></a>

### 03 · Convolution and Approximate Identities · 9 cards

*Convolution as filtering and as smoothing, the kernels that do it, and what it does to densities and to spectra*

1. Convolution: slide, multiply, add, and the inequality that bounds the result
2. The convolution theorem: blending in time is multiplying in frequency
3. Approximate identities: a shrinking bump that smooths a signal without moving it
4. The Gaussian and Poisson kernels: heat spreading, and a disc's boundary values
5. Blurring and unblurring: why a blur is easy and undoing it is not
6. Correlation: sliding one signal against another to find a known shape in noise
7. Wiener-Khinchin: the power spectrum is the transform of the autocorrelation
8. Densities add by convolution: the law of a sum of independent quantities
9. Solving differential equations by transform: derivatives become multiplication

<a name="w20-s04"></a>

### 04 · Characteristic Functions and Probability · 9 cards

*Fourier analysis of probability laws: inversion, continuity, the central limit theorem, jump laws and option pricing*

1. Characteristic functions: the transform of a probability law, which always exists
2. Moments and cumulants: what differentiating the fingerprint at zero tells you
3. Levy's inversion formula: getting the law back out of its fingerprint
4. Levy's continuity theorem: when fingerprints converge, the laws converge too
5. The central limit theorem in four lines, once you have fingerprints
6. How big must n be: the Berry-Esseen bound and the skewness correction
7. Infinitely divisible laws: the Levy-Khintchine recipe of drift, wobble and jumps
8. Stable laws: the limits you reach when the variance is infinite
9. Pricing by transform: Carr-Madan turns a fingerprint into a ladder of option prices

<a name="w20-s05"></a>

### 05 · Wavelets and Time-Frequency · 9 cards

*Seeing when each frequency happens: windows, scales, filter banks, denoising and compression*

1. Short-time Fourier transform: run the transform on sliding windows
2. Continuous wavelet transform: compare the signal with stretched and shifted wiggles
3. The Haar wavelet: averages and differences, and the ladder of coarse-to-fine views
4. Mallat's algorithm: every scale in linear time by filtering and halving
5. Filter banks: when splitting and rejoining returns the signal unchanged
6. Daubechies wavelets: short, smooth, and blind to polynomial trends
7. Wavelet denoising: keep the big coefficients, throw the small ones away
8. Compression by wavelets: why a few coefficients carry a whole picture
9. Frames in outline: redundant building blocks that still rebuild a signal stably

<a name="w20-s06"></a>

### 06 · Maximal Functions and Beyond · 6 cards

*The tools of modern harmonic analysis, proved where the proof is short and stated where it is deep*

1. The maximal function: the biggest average around a point, and the bound it obeys
2. Calderon-Zygmund decomposition: halve the boxes until the average is just big enough
3. Singular integrals: kernels that only just fail to be integrable, yet stay bounded
4. Interpolation: bounds at two sizes give bounds at every size between
5. Bochner's theorem: which functions are fingerprints, and which are spectra
6. Restriction and Kakeya in outline: what a spectrum living on a sphere can tell you

<a name="w21"></a>

## 21 · Algebraic and analytic number theory

Prove the prime number theorem with an error term, state the Riemann hypothesis precisely and say exactly what is proved around it, follow Dirichlet's theorem and the sieve results, and do Galois theory. Compute in a ring of algebraic integers and read the statements of Fermat, Birch-Swinnerton-Dyer and abc as mathematics rather than folklore.

8 shelves · **0 of 72 cards ready to read** · all planned

| Shelf | Cards |
| --- | ---: |
| [01 · Arithmetic Functions Again](#w21-s01) | 9 |
| [02 · The Zeta Function and the Prime Number Theorem](#w21-s02) | 10 |
| [03 · Characters and L-functions](#w21-s03) | 8 |
| [04 · Sieves and Prime Gaps](#w21-s04) | 8 |
| [05 · Fields and Galois Theory](#w21-s05) | 10 |
| [06 · Algebraic Numbers](#w21-s06) | 10 |
| [07 · Diophantine and Modular](#w21-s07) | 10 |
| [08 · Additive Combinatorics and Probabilistic Number Theory](#w21-s08) | 7 |

<a name="w21-s01"></a>

### 01 · Arithmetic Functions Again · 9 cards

*Multiplicative functions and Dirichlet convolution, Mobius inversion, average orders, partial summation, and the first genuine bounds on the prime count*

1. Big O and twiddle: how to say 'about this size' so the claim can be proved
2. Multiplicative functions: rules that split across coprime parts, and how to combine them
3. The Mobius function: plus one, minus one or zero, and how it undoes a divisor sum
4. The von Mangoldt function: weighting prime powers by logs so the sums behave
5. Average orders: the typical divisor count, found by summing along a hyperbola
6. Partial summation: trading a weighted sum for an integral against a running total
7. Squarefree numbers: why six in every ten integers are divisible by no square
8. Chebyshev's bounds: trapping the prime count between two multiples of x over log x
9. Mertens' theorems: the reciprocals of the primes add up like log log x

<a name="w21-s02"></a>

### 02 · The Zeta Function and the Prime Number Theorem · 10 cards

*Zeta as an analytic object, its functional equation and zero-free region, Perron's formula, the prime number theorem with an error term, and what the Riemann hypothesis would add*

1. Zeta of a complex variable: the Euler product, the pole at one, and the log derivative
2. The functional equation: zeta at s against zeta at one minus s, proved
3. The zero-free region: why zeta cannot vanish on the edge of the critical strip
4. Perron's formula: a running total recovered from its series by a contour integral
5. The prime number theorem: the full proof, from zeta's zeros to the count x over log x
6. The logarithmic integral: a far closer fit than x over log x, and where it too fails
7. The explicit formula: the prime count written exactly, one wave per zero of zeta
8. Counting the zeros: how many lie below a given height, and how tightly they pack
9. The Riemann hypothesis: the exact statement, and three equivalents worth knowing
10. Zeros on the line: what is proved, from Hardy to a positive proportion

<a name="w21-s03"></a>

### 03 · Characters and L-functions · 8 cards

*Characters as periodic multiplicative patterns, their L-functions, primes in progressions, Gauss sums and quadratic reciprocity*

1. Dirichlet characters: periodic multiplicative patterns that pick out one remainder class
2. Quadratic residues: which numbers are squares on a prime clock, and Euler's test
3. Dirichlet L-functions: one series per character, each with its own Euler product
4. Dirichlet's theorem: a progression coprime to its step holds infinitely many primes
5. Siegel-Walfisz: the prime count in a progression, uniform over small moduli
6. Gauss sums: weighted sums over roots of unity, with a size you can pin down exactly
7. Quadratic reciprocity: swapping the two primes, proved with a Gauss sum
8. The Jacobi symbol: extending the symbol to odd moduli so it computes without factoring

<a name="w21-s04"></a>

### 04 · Sieves and Prime Gaps · 8 cards

*Sieving from Eratosthenes to Selberg, the large sieve and Bombieri-Vinogradov, bounded and record gaps, Goldbach, and the four Landau problems*

1. Sieving by inclusion-exclusion: Legendre's count, and the error that swamps it
2. Brun's sieve and Selberg's: truncating inclusion-exclusion so the error stays small
3. The large sieve: how concentrated a set can be across many remainder classes at once
4. Bombieri-Vinogradov: primes spread evenly across classes, on average over the moduli
5. Bounded gaps: from pairs within 70 million to pairs within 246
6. Large gaps: the longest prime-free run, and the coin-flip model that predicts it
7. Goldbach: every even number as two primes, and how far that is actually proved
8. Landau's four problems: the questions about primes that are all still open

<a name="w21-s05"></a>

### 05 · Fields and Galois Theory · 10 cards

*Degree and towers, splitting fields, the Galois correspondence, solvability by radicals, finite fields, and what a straightedge and compass cannot do*

1. Field extensions: adding a number to a field, and measuring the result as a dimension
2. Minimal polynomials: the simplest equation a new number satisfies, and its other roots
3. Splitting fields: the smallest field where a polynomial factors all the way down
4. Normal and separable: holding every conjugate, and having no repeated roots
5. The Galois correspondence: subfields on one side, subgroups of symmetries on the other
6. Computing a Galois group: discriminant tests, resolvents and reduction modulo a prime
7. Solvable by radicals: why the cubic has a formula and the general quintic cannot
8. Cyclotomic fields: the nth roots of one, with the invertible remainders as symmetries
9. Finite fields: exactly one of each prime power size, with Frobenius as its symmetry
10. Straightedge and compass: why trisection and doubling the cube must fail

<a name="w21-s06"></a>

### 06 · Algebraic Numbers · 10 cards

*Algebraic integers, norms traces and discriminants, factorisation of ideals, class groups, units, ramification, and the p-adic view*

1. Algebraic integers: the numbers inside a bigger field that behave like whole numbers
2. Norm, trace and discriminant: collapsing an algebraic number into ordinary integers
3. Rings of integers: finding an integral basis, and the surprise when the root is 1 mod 4
4. Ideals factor uniquely even where numbers do not
5. Class groups: measuring how badly factorisation fails, and how far you must search
6. The class number formula: an analytic limit that counts the failure of factorisation
7. The unit theorem: how many independent invertible elements a ring of integers holds
8. Splitting and ramification: what becomes of an ordinary prime in a bigger ring
9. Chebotarev: what share of the primes split in each possible pattern
10. The p-adic numbers: closeness by divisibility, and lifting a root one power at a time

<a name="w21-s07"></a>

### 07 · Diophantine and Modular · 10 cards

*Pell and continued fractions, sums of squares and powers, descent, rational points on cubics, modular forms, and the statements of Fermat, BSD and abc*

1. Pell's equation: why the continued fraction of a square root gives every solution
2. Two squares: a prime is a sum of two squares exactly when it is 1 mod 4
3. Four squares and beyond: every integer as four squares, and how many cubes it takes
4. Fermat's last theorem: the descent for n equals 4, and the ideals beyond it
5. Rational points on a cubic: a finitely generated group with a rank nobody can always find
6. Birch-Swinnerton-Dyer: a curve's rank read off its L-function
7. Modular forms: so symmetric on the half plane that the coefficients count things
8. Modularity: every rational elliptic curve is a modular form, and Fermat follows
9. The abc conjecture: sums whose prime factors are all small are rare
10. Chasing an algebraic number with fractions: how close you can get, from Liouville to Roth

<a name="w21-s08"></a>

### 08 · Additive Combinatorics and Probabilistic Number Theory · 7 cards

*Prime factor counts as a random variable, equidistribution, long progressions, sum-product, and problems attacked by computer*

1. Typical numbers have about log log n distinct prime factors
2. Erdos-Kac: the count of prime factors follows the bell curve
3. Equidistribution: when fractional parts spread evenly, tested by exponential sums
4. Three distances: stepping by a fixed irrational leaves gaps of only three lengths
5. Long progressions: dense sets must contain them, and so must the primes
6. Sum-product: a set of numbers cannot be small for adding and for multiplying at once
7. Erdos problems: cheap to state, hard to settle, and often attacked by computer

## 22 · Algebraic geometry

Compute with polynomial systems: choose a term order, run Buchberger's algorithm, eliminate variables, and count the solutions of a finite system. Read a variety in affine and projective space, work with a plane curve and its singularities, do arithmetic on an elliptic curve up to the statements of Mordell-Weil and Birch-Swinnerton-Dyer, and state the Hodge conjecture precisely.

6 shelves · **0 of 54 cards ready to read** · all planned

| Shelf | Cards |
| --- | ---: |
| [01 · Polynomial Systems](#w22-s01) | 9 |
| [02 · Affine and Projective Varieties](#w22-s02) | 9 |
| [03 · Plane Curves](#w22-s03) | 9 |
| [04 · Elliptic Curves](#w22-s04) | 10 |

<a name="w22-s01"></a>

### 01 · Polynomial Systems · 9 cards

*Ideals, term orders, Grobner bases, elimination, and the two theorems that make polynomial algebra finite*

1. Ideals and varieties: the equations you may add, and the points that survive them
2. Monomial orders: ranking terms so that division in several variables has an answer
3. Grobner bases: the generating set that makes division give a definite answer
4. Normal forms: deciding whether one equation is a consequence of the others
5. Elimination: removing variables to solve a system or to find a curve's equation
6. The resultant: one determinant that says when two polynomials share a root
7. Finitely many solutions: counting them first, then finding them all
8. Hilbert's basis theorem: every polynomial system, however large, is really a finite one
9. The Nullstellensatz: over the complex numbers the points know the equations up to roots

<a name="w22-s02"></a>

### 02 · Affine and Projective Varieties · 9 cards

*Varieties in affine and projective space, the maps between them, dimension, tangent spaces and smoothness*

1. Affine varieties: the zero set of a system, and its ring of polynomial functions
2. The Zariski topology: calling solution sets closed, and splitting a shape into pieces
3. Projective space: adding points at infinity so that parallel lines meet
4. Projective varieties: equations of one degree, and the closure of an affine shape
5. Maps between varieties: polynomial maps, and ratios with a bad set
6. Birational equivalence: the same away from a small bad set, and the field that detects it
7. Dimension and tangent space: how many directions a shape has at a point
8. Smooth and singular points: the Jacobian test for a clean tangent direction
9. Degree: how many points a slice meets, and the polynomial that records it

<a name="w22-s03"></a>

### 03 · Plane Curves · 9 cards

*Curves in the plane: how they meet, how they fail to be smooth, how many holes they have, and the functions on them*

1. Conics: every smooth degree-two curve is a line in disguise
2. Intersection multiplicity: how tightly two curves touch where they meet
3. Bezout's theorem: two curves meet in exactly the product of their degrees
4. Nodes and cusps: the standard ways a curve fails to be smooth
5. Blowing up: replacing a point by all the directions through it to pull branches apart
6. Genus: the number of holes, and how degree and singular points fix it
7. Divisors and Riemann-Roch: counting the functions with the poles you prescribe
8. Riemann surfaces: a complex curve is a surface, and a doughnut is a rolled-up plane
9. Inflections and Cayley-Bacharach: nine points on a cubic, and the ninth for free

<a name="w22-s04"></a>

### 04 · Elliptic Curves · 10 cards

*The group law on a cubic, arithmetic over Q and over finite fields, the two big theorems, and the cryptography*

1. The group law: three points on a line add up to zero
2. Weierstrass form: the standard cubic, its discriminant and its j-invariant
3. Adding points by formula: slopes, the doubling case, and big multiples fast
4. Torsion points: the ones that return to zero, and how to list them all
5. The curve mod p: counting its points, and Hasse's bound on the count
6. Mordell-Weil: every rational point comes from a finite list of them
7. The L-function: every prime's point count packed into one series
8. Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer: the rank equals the order of vanishing at s equals 1
9. Curves for keys: the discrete logarithm problem, and counting points to pick a safe curve
10. What curves answer: congruent numbers, and the modularity theorem

<a name="w22-s05"></a>

### 05 · Cohomology and the Hodge Conjecture · 8 cards

*Cohomology in outline for varieties, the Hodge decomposition, cycle classes, and one Clay problem stated exactly*

1. Sheaves: data that exists locally, glued together with bookkeeping
2. De Rham cohomology: closed forms modulo exact ones, counted degree by degree
3. Cohomology of projective space: one class in every even degree
4. Complex manifolds and Kahler forms: holomorphic charts and a metric that fits them
5. Hodge decomposition: cohomology of a compact Kahler manifold splits by type
6. Line bundles and divisors: a codimension-one shape as a cohomology class
7. Algebraic cycles: subvarieties added with weights, and the classes they leave behind
8. The Hodge conjecture: every rational class of type p and p comes from a cycle

<a name="w22-s06"></a>

### 06 · Schemes and Modern Language · 9 cards

*The language the subject is written in now: Spec, schemes, morphisms, modules, moduli, and what computation can do*

1. Domains, principal ideals and unique factorisation: how the good rings nest
2. The spectrum of a ring: prime ideals as points, so every ring becomes a space
3. Localisation: inverting chosen functions to zoom in on one point
4. Morphisms of schemes: ring maps read backwards, and fibres as products
5. Modules: vector spaces over a ring, and the classification when the ring is a PID
6. Smith normal form: reducing an integer matrix until the module is readable off it
7. Moduli spaces: a space whose points are the shapes of a given kind
8. Algebraic statistics: a model is a variety, identifiability a question about ideals
9. Tropical geometry and computation: curves become polygons, and bases can blow up

Use a Lie group as a symmetry, optimise on a curved constraint set, and read the Yang-Mills mass-gap problem as a precise statement rather than a slogan.

<a name="w23-s01"></a>

### 01 · Curves · 9 cards

*how a path bends and twists, and why bending alone fixes its shape*

1. Regular curve and arc length: relabel a path by distance travelled
2. Curvature: how fast the direction of travel turns
3. Osculating circle: the circle that hugs a curve best
4. Frenet-Serret frame: the axes a traveller carries along the curve
5. Torsion: how fast a curve twists out of its own plane
6. Curvature and torsion at any speed: formulas that skip relabelling
7. Fundamental theorem of curves: bending and twisting fix the shape
8. Turning and winding numbers: whole turns a closed loop makes
9. Isoperimetric and four-vertex: two theorems the circle wins

<a name="w23-s02"></a>

### 02 · Surfaces · 10 cards

*curvature of a surface, measured from outside and felt from inside*

1. Regular surface: a shape that looks like a flat patch up close
2. First fundamental form: measuring lengths and angles inside a surface
3. Surface area: integrate the surface's own measuring rule
4. Gauss map and second fundamental form: how a surface leaves its tangent plane
5. Principal, mean and Gaussian curvature: two bending rates and their combinations
6. Point types: dome, saddle, trough and flat
7. Minimal surface: zero mean curvature and the shape a soap film takes
8. Theorema Egregium: Gaussian curvature can be felt from inside
9. Geodesic on a surface: the straightest path an inhabitant can walk
10. Gauss-Bonnet: total curvature counts the holes

<a name="w23-s03"></a>

### 03 · Manifolds · 10 cards

*calculus on a space that is flat only up close*

1. Smooth manifold: coordinate patches that agree where they overlap
2. Sphere, torus and projective plane: the shapes every definition is tested on
3. Tangent space and differential: velocities, and how a map carries them
4. Diffeomorphism, immersion, submersion: the ways one manifold maps into another
5. Covectors and tensor fields: gadgets that eat directions and return numbers
6. Regular value theorem: solution sets come out smooth for free
7. Vector field and its flow: follow the arrows and you get a motion
8. Lie bracket: what changes when you swap the order of two motions
9. Partition of unity: glue local work into one global object
10. Sard's theorem and degree: bad values are rare, wrapping is a whole number

<a name="w23-s04"></a>

### 04 · Differential Forms · 10 cards

*one derivative and one boundary theorem behind all of vector calculus*

1. Differential form: a signed measurement of the box that directions span
2. Pullback: carry a form backwards through a map
3. Exterior derivative: one rule containing gradient, curl and divergence
4. Closed and exact forms: when a potential exists
5. Integration on an oriented manifold: patch the pieces together consistently
6. Stokes on manifolds: a derivative inside equals the thing itself on the edge
7. Green, divergence and curl theorems: three results, one statement
8. de Rham cohomology: closed forms that are not derivatives count holes
9. Hodge star: Maxwell's equations in two lines
10. Symplectic form: the two-form behind Hamiltonian mechanics

<a name="w23-s05"></a>

### 05 · Riemannian Geometry · 10 cards

*a metric, the one connection it forces, and what curvature does to shortest paths*

1. Riemannian metric: lengths and angles at every point
2. Levi-Civita connection: the one derivative rule a metric forces
3. Parallel transport: carry a vector without turning it
4. Geodesic and exponential map: shoot a straight line from a point in a direction
5. Sphere, plane and hyperbolic space: the three reference geometries
6. Riemann curvature tensor: what is left when two derivatives fail to swap
7. Ricci and scalar curvature: averaged curvature and how volumes grow
8. Volume form and Laplace-Beltrami: integrate and diffuse on a curved space
9. Jacobi field: curvature seen as nearby paths focusing or spreading
10. Hopf-Rinow: when a shortest path is guaranteed to exist

<a name="w23-s06"></a>

### 06 · Lie Groups · 10 cards

*continuous symmetry as a group that is also a manifold*

1. Lie group: a group that is also a smooth manifold
2. Lie algebra: the tangent space at the identity, and exp back into the group
3. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff: two small motions leave a bracket behind
4. Rotation group: three-dimensional turns and the four-number shortcut
5. Representation: a symmetry group written as matrices acting on a space
6. Group action and homogeneous space: a space that looks the same from everywhere
7. Killing field: a direction in which the geometry does not change
8. Noether: every continuous symmetry hands you a conserved quantity
9. Hairy ball theorem: a field on a sphere must stand still somewhere
10. Principal bundle and connection: a symmetry group attached at every point

<a name="w23-s07"></a>

### 07 · Geometric Analysis and Physics · 10 cards

*curvature that evolves, curvature as gravity, and geometry put to work on data*

1. Ricci flow: let curvature spread out like heat
2. Einstein equations: curvature on one side, matter on the other
3. Yang-Mills: a connection whose curvature costs energy
4. Mass gap: the Yang-Mills prize problem stated precisely
5. Riemannian gradient descent: steps that stay on the curved set
6. Riemannian Hessian: second-order steps on a curved set
7. Covariance matrices: a curved space you can average inside
8. Frechet mean: the average of points on a curved space
9. Fisher metric: a family of models as a curved space
10. Manifold learning: coordinates for data lying near a curved sheet

## 24 · Computability and complexity

Define computation precisely, prove that some problems cannot be solved at all and others cannot be solved fast under standard assumptions, and read P versus NP as a precise statement with a clear account of what is proved around it. Follow randomised, quantum and interactive models well enough to say what each one buys, and read category-theoretic language when it appears.

<a name="w24-s01"></a>

### 01 · Models of Computation · 10 cards

*machines with fixed memory, with a stack and with a tape; what computation means; the first impossibility results*

1. Finite automata: fixed memory reads once and decides accept or reject
2. Guessing machines: allowed every move at once, and no more powerful
3. Regular expressions: patterns and machines describe the same string sets
4. Pumping and Myhill-Nerode: proving fixed memory is not enough
5. Grammars and a stack: machines that handle nesting
6. The language ladder: more memory buys strictly more patterns
7. Turing machines: a tape, a head and a table of rules
8. Church-Turing thesis: every reasonable machine computes the same things
9. Universal machines: one runs them all, and cannot predict them
10. Rice's theorem: no interesting property of a program is decidable

<a name="w24-s02"></a>

### 02 · Computability and Logic · 8 cards

*decidable against semidecidable, incompleteness, undecidable equations, shortest-program information, and machine-checked proof*

1. Decidable and semidecidable: always an answer, or only when the answer is yes
2. Busy beaver: a function that outgrows everything computable
3. Godel's theorems: arithmetic cannot settle everything true about itself
4. Hilbert's tenth problem: no test for whole-number solutions
5. Kolmogorov complexity: the length of the shortest program that prints it
6. Incompressibility: almost every file is its own shortest description
7. Lambda calculus: computing with nothing but functions
8. Proof assistants: a machine checks every step of a proof

<a name="w24-s03"></a>

### 03 · Time Complexity · 9 cards

*cost classes, P and NP, reductions and completeness, Cook-Levin, the classes above NP, and strict hierarchies*

1. Time and space classes: cost measured against how the input grows
2. P and NP: solving quickly against checking quickly
3. Reductions: convert one problem into another to compare difficulty
4. Cook-Levin: satisfiability is the first universally hardest problem
5. Karp's list: a small kit of reductions that reaches everything
6. Pseudo-polynomial time: fast only while the numbers stay small
7. SAT solvers: hard in theory, often easy on the instance in hand
8. Above NP: alternating there-exists and for-all, and memory instead of time
9. Hierarchy and Ladner: more time really helps, and middle ground must exist

<a name="w24-s04"></a>

### 04 · Beyond Worst Case · 8 cards

*approximation with guarantees, coin flips, a second parameter, average-case hardness, lower bounds, and the barriers around P versus NP*

1. Approximation: a guaranteed near-answer, and limits on the guarantee
2. Relaxation and rounding: solve a fractional version, then round it back
3. BPP: coin flips that buy speed at a controlled error rate
4. Pseudorandomness: fake coins that a fast test cannot tell from real ones
5. Parameterised complexity: hard in general, easy while one number stays small
6. Average-case hardness: hard on the inputs you actually draw
7. Circuit lower bounds: counting gates to prove nothing can be fast
8. P versus NP: the precise statement, and the barriers that block proof

<a name="w24-s05"></a>

### 05 · Algebraic, Interactive and Quantum · 7 cards

*gates and depth, counting rather than deciding, permanent against determinant, proof by conversation or spot check, and quantum circuits*

1. Boolean circuits: cost measured in gates and in depth
2. Counting: how many solutions, not merely whether one exists
3. Permanent against determinant: two near-identical formulas, wildly different cost
4. Interactive proofs: convincing a sceptic by conversation, or by spot checks
5. Qubits: a unit vector of amplitudes, read once and collapsed
6. BQP: what a quantum circuit does and does not speed up
7. Shor and Grover: period finding breaks RSA, search gains a square root

<a name="w24-s06"></a>

### 06 · Data, Learning and Fine-Grained Complexity · 6 cards

*one-pass summaries, random projection, bits across a gap, how many examples are enough, regret, and hardness inside P*

1. Streaming: one pass, tiny memory, an answer with error bars
2. Random projection: squash the data, keep the distances
3. Communication complexity: how many bits must cross the gap
4. Learning with a guarantee: how many examples are enough
5. Regret: judged against the best single choice in hindsight
6. Fine-grained complexity: why quadratic is probably the floor

<a name="w24-s07"></a>

### 07 · Category Theory in Outline · 9 cards

*categories and functors, naturality, universal properties, adjunctions, monoids and monads, folds, and side-by-side composition*

1. Categories and functors: objects, arrows, and maps that respect them
2. Commutative diagrams: every route agrees, and every arrow can be reversed
3. Natural transformations and Yoneda: an object is known by its arrows
4. Universal properties: defined by the one arrow that always exists
5. Pullbacks and pushouts: matching pairs, and gluing along a shared part
6. Adjunctions: two functors whose arrows correspond exactly
7. Monoids and monads: combining values, and sequencing steps that can fail
8. F-algebras: one step of a recursive structure, then folded away
9. Monoidal categories: things side by side, drawn as parallel wires

[↑ Back to all wings](#syllabus)

<a name="w25"></a>

## 25 · Frontier

State each open problem exactly, say what is proved around it and what is not, tell a claim from an accepted proof from a formalisation, and follow a route of earlier cards to the point where the research literature starts. Nothing here promises that reading equips anyone to solve a problem; it promises the reader will know precisely what the problem is.

7 shelves · **0 of 43 cards ready to read** · all planned

| Shelf | Cards |
| --- | ---: |
| [01 · How Research Works](#w25-s01) | 7 |
| [02 · The Millennium Problems](#w25-s02) | 7 |
| [03 · Primes and Numbers](#w25-s03) | 7 |
| [04 · Geometry and Combinatorics](#w25-s04) | 6 |
| [05 · Analysis and Dynamics](#w25-s05) | 6 |
| [06 · Algebra, Logic and Computation](#w25-s06) | 5 |
| [07 · Physics, Finance and Machines](#w25-s07) | 5 |

<a name="w25-s01"></a>

### 01 · How Research Works · 7 cards

*The habits of research mathematics: proof, conjecture, counterexample, statement, paper, machine, and how to start on an open problem*

1. Proof: a general argument, not a pile of checked cases
2. Kinds of claim: theorem, conjecture, model, approximation, and the status each carries
3. Counterexamples: one failure refutes a universal claim, and a million successes prove nothing
4. Reading a statement: quantifiers, domain, hypotheses, conclusion, and the boundary cases
5. Reading a paper: find the theorem, compare it with the abstract, trace what it depends on
6. Machines in proofs: numerical evidence, symbolic computation, exhaustive search, and proof assistants
7. Working on a problem: small cases, the literature, the status ledger, and knowing when you are wrong

<a name="w25-s02"></a>

### 02 · The Millennium Problems · 7 cards

*Each problem stated exactly, with its hypotheses, what it does not claim, what is known, and the route that reaches it*

1. The Riemann hypothesis: every nontrivial zero of zeta has real part one half
2. Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer: the rank of an elliptic curve equals the order of vanishing of its L-function
3. P versus NP: is checking a solution really easier than finding one
4. Navier-Stokes: do smooth solutions of the fluid equations exist for all time in three dimensions
5. The Hodge conjecture: which cohomology classes come from algebraic subvarieties
6. Yang-Mills existence and mass gap: build the quantum theory rigorously and show its lightest particle has positive mass
7. The Poincare conjecture, solved: a simply connected closed three-manifold is a sphere

<a name="w25-s03"></a>

### 03 · Primes and Numbers · 7 cards

*The open and recently settled questions about primes and integers, and the ones with prizes attached*

1. Twin primes: infinitely many primes two apart, and the bounded gap of 246 that is proved
2. Goldbach: every even number above 2 is a sum of two primes
3. Landau's four problems: Goldbach, twin primes, Legendre, and primes of the form n squared plus 1
4. The abc conjecture: when a plus b equals c, the radical of abc is rarely much smaller than c
5. Beal's conjecture: A to the x plus B to the y equals C to the z with exponents above 2 forces a common factor
6. Fermat's last theorem, proved in 1995 and reported formalised in 2026: what a formalisation adds
7. Erdos problems: a database of hundreds, with prizes, and the ones a computer search can touch

<a name="w25-s04"></a>

### 04 · Geometry and Combinatorics · 6 cards

*Open problems you can draw, some recently moved by computer search*

1. The unit-distance problem: how many pairs of n points can be exactly one apart
2. Kakeya: a set containing a unit segment in every direction, and how small it can be
3. Colouring the plane: how many colours so that no two points at distance one share a colour
4. Cycle double cover: every bridgeless graph has cycles covering each edge exactly twice
5. Sunflowers and union-closed families: two set-system problems with recent progress
6. Sphere packing, solved in dimensions 8 and 24: Viazovska's modular-form proof

<a name="w25-s05"></a>

### 05 · Analysis and Dynamics · 6 cards

*Open questions about equations and iterations, including the 2026 Navier-Stokes claim*

1. The September 2026 Navier-Stokes claim: forced finite-time blow-up for versions C and D
2. Collatz: halve if even, triple and add one if odd, and always reach 1
3. The August 2026 zeta-zeros claim: a reported rise in the proportion of zeros on the line from 41.6 to 67.2 percent
4. The Jacobian conjecture: a polynomial map with constant nonzero Jacobian has a polynomial inverse
5. Two algebra questions: is every group sofic, and is the sum of two nil left ideals nil (Kothe)
6. Percolation: when does a random lattice connect, and the reported 'dying percolation' result to verify

<a name="w25-s06"></a>

### 06 · Algebra, Logic and Computation · 5 cards

*Open problems where algebra meets logic and computation*

1. The inverse Galois problem: is every finite group the Galois group of some extension of the rationals
2. The continuum hypothesis: no size between the integers and the reals, and why it cannot be settled
3. The unique games conjecture: one hardness assumption that would settle many approximation questions
4. Graph isomorphism: are two drawings the same graph, and Babai's quasipolynomial algorithm
5. Hilbert's and Smale's lists: the problems that shaped a century, and the ones still open

<a name="w25-s07"></a>

### 07 · Physics, Finance and Machines · 5 cards

*Where the frontier touches physics, finance and artificial intelligence*

1. Quantum advantage: what BQP can do that classical machines cannot, as far as anyone knows
2. Machines that prove: what AI systems reported proving or formalising in 2026, and how to audit such a claim
3. Open questions in finance mathematics: rough volatility, the smile's true dynamics, and what a market model cannot know
4. Optimal transport: moving mass at least cost, and the theory questions behind modern learning
5. The status register: every problem in this wing, its status, its date, and its source
